Nov. 10, 1861: The birth of Robert Innes

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Born Nov. 10, 1861, in Scotland, Robert Thorburn Ayton Innes would reshape our understanding of our cosmic neighborhood when he discovered our nearest stellar neighbor, Proxima Centauri, in 1912. Innes suspected that Alpha Centauri, a binary system made up of Alpha Centauri A (Rigil Kentaurus) and Alpha Centauri B (Toliman) and then thought to be the nearest stars to Earth (besides the Sun) – had a third member.

The director of the Union Observatory in Johannesburg, South Africa, Innes began a systematic survey for the missing companion star, comparing five years’ worth of photographic plates using a blink-microscope. He noted that a small, faint, red star seemed to move in concert with Alpha Centauri; further research revealed it was in fact closer to Earth than its companions. Innes announced the find in 1915, and later named the body Proxima Centauri – proxima meaning “nearest” in Latin.

Elisa Neckar is senior production editor of Astronomy magazine, and the editor of the Today in the History of Astronomy feature on Astronomy.com.

Circle Makes the Case for AI Agent Payments, With Spending Controls at the Center

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Circle is arguing that stablecoins could become a useful payment layer for AI agents that buy software, access services or settle transactions on behalf of people and businesses. A November 4 explainer from the USDC issuer sets out what it calls agentic commerce, a concept that would allow authorized software to initiate purchases within predefined rules.

Circle is arguing that stablecoins could become a useful payment layer for AI agents that buy software, access services or settle transactions on behalf of people and businesses. A November 4 explainer from the USDC issuer sets out what it calls agentic commerce, a concept that would allow authorized software to initiate purchases within predefined rules. It is a vision for a developing market, not evidence that an autonomous AI payments network has already reached mass adoption.

The basic idea is to let a software agent complete a purchase after receiving instructions and permission from a human or business. An agent might compare pricing, obtain a computing service, pay a provider and record the transaction without asking a person to click through each step. This differs from a chatbot that only recommends products or from an automatic recurring subscription. Stablecoins are appealing in this setting because value can move over internet-native rails and settle across borders, but those features are useful only when an agent can prove what it is authorized to do.

The central design challenge is delegated authority. A business needs to specify which agent may spend, how much, on which categories of supplier and for what period. It also needs authentication, records of the instruction that triggered a payment and a way to revoke authority. Without those safeguards, automated payments can turn minor software errors or malicious prompts into real financial losses. Circle's discussion points to the infrastructure opportunity, but a stablecoin transfer alone cannot determine whether the underlying purchase was legitimate or met the user's expectations.

USDC and other dollar-linked tokens could allow small cross-border payments and programmable transfers that are difficult to reproduce with traditional card infrastructure. However, blockchain transfers can be difficult to reverse. Conventional card payments often involve dispute rights, fraud monitoring and customer-support processes that do not automatically accompany an onchain transfer. A workable agent payment service therefore needs explicit handling for refunds, incorrect goods, failed delivery and disputes. It must also address sanctions checks, identity requirements and jurisdictional limits where relevant. Technology that moves money quickly cannot replace those business obligations.

Developers are experimenting with AI systems that interact with tools, APIs and online marketplaces. As those systems gain the ability to perform tasks rather than simply produce text, a reliable method of paying for digital services becomes more relevant. Stablecoin issuers and payment processors see potential demand, but practical uptake depends on merchants accepting the method and users trusting the controls. The most credible demonstrations will involve narrow, verifiable use cases rather than broad claims that intelligent agents can safely manage unlimited funds.

Payment providers will need to show who bears liability when an agent makes a mistaken purchase, how permissions are audited and how customers recover from fraud. They will also need to show cost advantages after fees, compliance and integration work are included. Circle's essay is best read as an articulation of a developing architecture, not a new product launch. The long-term test is whether automated payments become easier for ordinary businesses without making financial control harder.

Primary source: Circle analysis of agentic commerce. For additional context, see NewsBTC’s report on a consumer-facing USDC payment integration.

Mini Review: Pikmin 4 - Nintendo Switch 2 Edition + Dandori Academy (Switch 2) - Pleasant But Perplexingly Piecemeal

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The latest addition to the pantheon of obscenely long-titled Nintendo Switch 2 Editions is a strange one. Pikmin 4 - Nintendo Switch 2 Edition + Dandori Academy takes the Switch 1 game that I have long considered ripe for a new hardware touch-up and does...not a lot with it. It's a chance to revisit one of Nintendo's finest games from the last generation, and the upgrade's price of admission (£7.99 / $9.99) is admittedly a small one, but on the 'new' front, I'm left wanting more.

Let's start with the usual 'Switch 2 Edition' bells and whistles. Pikmin 4 now plays in stunning native 4K resolution docked, and there's added GameShare support for those who want to play some cross-console Dandori Battles with a pal.

If you ask me, Pikmin 4 was already one of the best-looking titles on Switch 1, and the resolution bump doesn't feel transformative. That said, there's no denying that it's even more of a looker on the newer hardware.

Review Pikmin 4 (Switch) - Relaxed, Refined Real-Time Strategy, Ready To Crack The Mainstream

Strangely, the typical Switch 2 givens of a 60fps boost and Mouse Mode are not included this time around. While the former is disappointing, the latter downright confuses me. Nintendo has crowbarred this control scheme in everywhere, but it's absent from a game with a stick-controlled pointer? Even in the co-op Pebble Pitcher mode? Madness!

There's also a new Howl-Out feature that lets you shout commands to Oatchi via the Switch 2 microphone. It's a sweet, if occasionally inconsistent, little bonus that I quickly turned off after realising my real-world dog was getting miffed at me shouting "Good Pup!" without giving him a stroke on the head.

The big newbie is the titular Dandori Academy, where you're tasked with clearing a stage's treasure as quickly as possible. You practice during the week, then take a weekend 'exam', unlocking various medals and in-game rewards in the process. The Dandori stages were a highlight of the base game, and it's great to have even more of them to play here. The three difficulty options provide a welcome challenge in the later stages, and with 72 of them arriving over the next year, it's a wonderful excuse to return to one of my favourite Switch 1 games.

The mode's rollout, however, is a little confusing. 72 new stages sound great on paper, but that means only six new challenges are available each month. If you happen to ace most of them on the first exam weekend, you're left with three weeks of nothing new. Sure, it gives me a prime reason to come back to Pikmin 4 on the regular in 2027, but it does leave things feeling rather slight at launch.

Overall, £8/$10 is a small price to pay for some minor new features and a fun little mode that'll keep me entertained over the coming year, but it's more of a budding shoot right now than the full-bloom upgrade I had dreamed of.

Here's What Xbox Owners Need To Know About 'Moving To New Zealand' For GTA 6

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

So, it's finally almost time for GTA 6. You've probably preloaded the game on your Xbox Series X and Xbox Series S already, and now you're eagerly awaiting the official launch that takes place on November 19th.

However, there are more than a few whispers going around about the "New Zealand" trick — something we've seen time and time again over the past decade-plus on Xbox — and it looks set to have a big influence again with GTA 6.

But what is it, and most importantly could it get you banned? Let's take a look at the facts:

On this page: Here's What Xbox Owners Need To Know About 'Moving To New Zealand' For GTA 6

A game like GTA 6 obviously has a set release date of November 19th, but it lands at "midnight local time" depending on the region you're in. That means New Zealand ends up being the first region to get access, and their "midnight" corresponds to 3am PT / 6am ET / 11am GMT on November 18th.

Therefore, if people want to play the game as early as possible, they'll need to be in New Zealand - and the "New Zealand Trick" refers to changing your region in your console's settings, providing slightly early access to GTA 6.

There's always been a lot of chatter online about how Xbox doesn't really care about you "moving to New Zealand" to get around release times, but that's technically not the case. Xbox's terms and conditions specifically state that trying to "circumvent regional content restrictions" is prohibited, and this is mentioned a few times in documentation:

"Attempting to circumvent restrictions can affect both customer privacy and the integrity of Microsoft or partner services" [...] "Make sure that you're only changing your country/region if: You've moved to a different country. You're already in a region different from the one that was set for your Microsoft account and/or XBOX console."

The official answer is yes - Xbox could take whatever kind of actions they wanted against an account that was found breaking the rules to play GTA 6 early on Xbox Series X and S. It could result in a warning, a suspension, a ban, or anything else they see fit.

You might also get away with it, as have the majority of people with many other games in the past, but it's still something you should only do at your own risk. We don't recommend breaking the rules just to play a game a few hours early, but we're also not naive enough to think that people won't be trying it come November 18th!

Something to also keep in mind with this is that spoilers will likely begin hitting the web from the New Zealand release time on November 18th (if not earlier). At that point, we expect all kinds of footage and screenshots to begin flooding social media, so you may want to have a bit of a digital detox until the 19th if you don't want to see anything.

Anyway, we know this is a hot topic, and feel free to share your thoughts about it in the comments below. The decision is up to you - but just know that you are running a risk by "moving to New Zealand", however small it might appear to be.

Trisha Paytas Is Finally Opening Up About the ‘Frenemies’ Split With Ethan Klein

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It’s been a rough few weeks for Ethan Klein. The cohost of The H3 Podcast made headlines in October after his wife and podcast co-host Hila Klein dropped bombshell news accusing him of having a six-month-long affair. “I am living in a nightmare I can’t wake up from,” she wrote on Instagram. “My husband has been having an affair. I have been fed lie after lie after lie for months. I am tired of lies.”

Now, the release of a memoir from his other former podcast cohost, Trisha Paytas, probably isn’t going to help.

Out today, Crying on the Kitchen Floor is a sprawling memoir, starting with Paytas’ birth in Riverside, California, and encompassing her early attempts at YouTube virality, sex work, and multi-faceted career as an online troll. But it’s also her first no-holds-barred recollection of the fallout of her podcast Frenemies, which she made with now-ousted H3 host Klein. (Paytas is married to Moses Hacmon — Hila’s brother — a marriage that complicated her already complex relationships with Klein.) In the book, Paytas says the Frenemies split started as a miscommunication and led to Klein waging all-out war against her — all in an effort to make more money. (Klein did not respond to Rolling Stone’s request for comment.)

After the allegations of infidelity broke online, the crew of The H3 Podcast released a statement saying they were “shocked, angry, and heartbroken,” and announced they would no longer work with Klein, placing the podcast on an indefinite hiatus. Klein, 41, did not outright confirm the allegation but announced he would “remove [his] ownership” from H3 and all its associated podcasts and projects. “I know a lot of people are understandably looking for a comment from me, but I ask that you please respect my family’s privacy and do not contact them looking for me or asking for their comments,” Klein said. “They have nothing to do with this and share in your outrage and disappointment.” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Timing is everything. When the news first dropped, Paytas said she was “relieved” that H3 was finally over. But in Crying on the Kitchen Floor, Paytas gives her unbarred thoughts about her initial thoughts of Klein and why her own podcast with him came to an abrupt end.

“I’d believed that, no matter what superficial conflicts Ethan and I got into, at the end of the day, we were genuinely friends. I cared for him. I thought he cared for me too,” Paytas writes. “Maybe I should have known that as soon as I was no longer useful, Ethan would drop me. What I never expected was that he and Hila would try to destroy me.”

Paytas first appeared on H3 in 2019 as a guest. At the time, she was a legacy fixture on YouTube, but had struggled to solidify a steady following. Mostly, she used her social media accounts to troll other internet celebrities, discuss her work as a stripper and a sex worker, and promote her OnlyFans. In a past episode, Klein had dismissed her content as annoying, but fans responded well to her appearance. Paytas felt good about the invite, but less than impressed with their setup.

“Though the set itself was giving Howard Stern chic, the rest of the office felt very haphazard and amateurish, like a bunch of dudes were just pretending to run a media company. Which, as it turned out, wasn’t far from the truth,” she writes. “Ethan and I weren’t going to be best friends, but I recognized him as a kind of kindred spirit. At this core, he was a troll. He stirred the pot.”

His team contacted her again when they wanted to make a new podcast with a female co-host. In September 2020, Frenemies was born. It was a weekly podcast where Paytas and Klein could talk rapid-fire about any cultural topic that came to mind, but mostly riffed on their often-contentious relationship. When Paytas signed onto the show,  she agreed to a 55-45  revenue split on the main episodes and sponsors, allowing Klein to keep all the revenue on the H3 highlights channel. At the height of the series, Paytas writes that she was making $180,000 a month. But the highlights channel was making almost four times the podcast revenue — and Paytas claims she didn’t see a dollar.  Related Content Trisha Paytas Admits Feeling ‘Relief’ Over Ethan Klein’s ‘H3’ Exit After Cheating Scandal Ethan Klein Confirms He'll ‘Remove Ownership’ From ‘H3 Podcast’ After Wife Accuses Him of Cheating Hillary Clinton to Break Down the ‘High Stakes’ of the 2026 Midterms on New Podcast Hunter Biden Talks Crack, Family, and the Owl That Saved His Life

Things came to a head nine months into the show, when the two filmed the 39th episode. In the now infamous recording — which is still up on YouTube — Paytas and Klein start their usual sparring but it got out of hand. Paytas asked to stop filming, verging on tears, and accused Klein of “gaslighting” her over money.

In Crying on the Kitchen Floor, Paytas writes that she left the recording feeling overwhelmed, but assumed that she and Klein would work it out. When she posted on her Instagram that she was “stepping away” from Frenemies, she says, it was a joke. But she was shocked when Klein went live and blamed Paytas for the show’s problems — including a claim that she had attempted to get the crew fired, which she writes is entirely false. Ethan’s videos started a tidal wave of harassment against Paytas, which, she writes, triggered a mental health crisis that left her despondent and spiraling. But when the two managed to film a final episode of Frenemies, one Paytas describes as a “beautiful experience”; Klein refused to release it. Trending Stories Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ Pink Becomes First Solo Female Pop Artist to Headline the Sphere Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Trump Finally Gets Around to Claiming the Midterms Were Rigged

“Even though Ethan and I had been attacking each other like rabid dogs for the better part of three weeks, when we sat down for one final rodeo in the Frenemies studio, we were able to talk more as friends than enemies,” she writes. “After that last closure episode, we’d been so close to being okay. We’d squashed the beef but for some reason, a few days after recording, Ethan said he wasn’t going to release it. It seemed more likely to me that the real reason Ethan didn’t want to release the episode was because he realized keeping the beef going was better for business.”

Paytas writes that after recovering from the split, she’s now focused on living a better, less reactive life, for her husband and three children. “Ethan could only affect me if I let him,” she writes. “And goddamnit, I couldn’t give him that power anymore. Not if I wanted this life to be my own.”

Fremantle’s Wildstar & Filmmaker Dan O’Neill Team On Wildlife Venture

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: A natural history filmmaker and Fremantle label Wildstar have partnered on a venture in which travellers will head out on excursions while having their journey documented by a crew.

Dan O’Neill and luxury travel specialist Steppes Travel have launched Wildstar Expeditions with Fremantle’s Wildstar Films, which makes the likes of the Ryan Reynolds-narrated Underdogs for Nat Geo and Disney+.

Wildstar Expeditions will take travellers “seeking exceptional experiences” on a journey with O’Neill and a Wildstar film crew. The crew will document every stage of the trip, making a film of the experience that the guests can then watch afterwards. Related Stories Acquisitions Skydance-Owned 5 Buys UK Rights To 'Wentworth' Sequel Series Executives Fremantle Global Entertainment Exec Chris O'Dell Retires After 19 Years At 'Got Talent' Maker

O’Neill said: “What excites me about Wildstar Expeditions is being able to take people to places most people simply can’t access, using the same expedition planning and field logistics that make serious research and filming possible in difficult terrain. This is a completely new concept, and there’s something so exciting about inviting people to be part of it from the very start, experiencing that access for the first time in this way.” Watch on Deadline

Wildstar COO Rebecca Wootton added: “This is more than a luxury holiday. We’re offering guests the rare opportunity to step behind the scenes of wildlife filmmaking, travelling alongside our award-winning film crews and expert guide Dan O’Neill. They’ll discover how some of the world’s most celebrated natural history programmes are made, gain unique insights from leading filmmakers and conservationists, and experience extraordinary wildlife encounters in places few people will ever visit.

The group comprises multiple members of current leadership including Chief Executive Michael Watts; Elizabeth Lindsey, Global President, Brands and Properties; Jason Ranne, President, Global Talent Representation; Kevin Shivers, Co-President, Music; Matthew Vandrau, President, EMEA & APAC; Elena Rochelli, Chief Financial Officer; and Jon Liebman, Chairman and CEO of Brillstein Entertainment Partners. Related Stories News Wasserman Buyout Moves Forward With Investment From Providence Acquisitions Wasserman Rebrand Full Steam Ahead As Formal Bidding For Embattled LA28 Chief's Empire Begins

Each will continue serving their leadership role within their respective business units.

They are joined by Davis Noell, Senior Managing Director, Co-Head of North America; Greg Brown, Operating Partner; Scott Marimow, Managing Director; and Ahana Maken, Senior Vice President at Providence Equity Partners. Watch on Deadline

Providence, the company’s majority owner, will name additional outside board members in the coming months.

Wasserman formally stepped down from his namesake management company last summer when he sold his stake in the business to Providence Equity, adding to its 60% holding. It was rebranded The Team. The deal followed an auction process that saw a number of Hollywood players kick the tires from WME, CAA, Range Media Partners, UTA, Goldman Sachs and a smattering of other private equity firms.

“This is an important moment for our company,” said CEO Watts. “The members of this board have shown the qualities we value most: embracing our culture, supporting their colleagues and delivering exceptional results for our clients. The company provides representation and marketing services to talent, brands and properties in sports music and entertainment across 28 countries and 70+ cities worldwide. Its sale process was precipitated after files related to Jeffrey Epstein revealed flirtatious emails Wasserman sent in the early 2000s to close Epstein associate Ghislaine Maxwell.

McTominay: ‘Napoli gave me the foundation to focus, and I have to thank Conte’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scott McTominay has thanked Antonio Conte for his role in his success at Napoli and described the club’s Scudetto-winning night as the best moment of his career.

According to Rivista Undici, via IlNapolista, the Scotland international has settled quickly into life in Italy since joining from Manchester United in 2024. He won the Scudetto and the Supercoppa Italiana under Conte.

“Napoli and Italy have given me the foundation I needed to focus on my work. Of course, I also have to thank Antonio Conte, who helped me a great deal. I had a very good relationship with him and the rest of the club from day one,” McTominay said. “We’re all a family: all the coaches, the kit men, the physios, the doctors.”

McTominay scored in the 2-0 win over Cagliari that clinched Napoli’s fourth league title in 2025, and the memory still gives him goosebumps.

“There was tension in the air, but when I think back to that game… I still get shivers today. Living through moments like that makes you want to achieve even more, because those are the ones you remember most fondly. It was without doubt one of the best days of my career. In fact, it was the best of all. I want to experience another night like that.”

The midfielder has become a hero for Napoli supporters, but he plays down the label.

“People shouldn’t see me that way. They should see me as someone who works hard, and that has nothing to do with football or what you achieve on the pitch. My aim is to come in, work, go home and rest.

“I think that throughout my career I’ve always risen to the occasion in big games, in the most important moments. That kind of pressure excites me.”

He also credited his father for his work ethic.

“If you don’t treat your career almost like an obsession, you’ll never reach the top level. What counts is what you do every day when you arrive at the training ground, and how you do things away from the pitch.”

From ‘Dil Se’ to ‘Ramayana’: How Indian Cinema Shattered the U.K., Ireland Box Office Glass Ceiling

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

When Mani Ratnam’s romantic thriller “Dil Se..” debuted in August 1998, it achieved what was then considered unthinkable for South Asian cinema: entering the official U.K. and Ireland top 10 box office chart at No. 8. Powered by Shah Rukh Khan’s burgeoning overseas stardom and A.R. Rahman’s ubiquitous soundtrack, the film proved that Indian releases could cut through mainstream British multiplexes on limited screens.

That watershed moment initiated a 28-year ascent across Bollywood and South Indian regional industries, culminating this weekend when Sony Pictures’ mythological epic “Ramayana: Rise of a Legend” debuted at No. 1 with £2.1 million ($2.8 million) including previews, officially claiming the territory’s box office summit. Related Stories Taylor Swift, Inducted Into Nashville Songwriters Hall of Fame, Says 'There Is No Music Community Like This Music Community. I Know, I Checked All the Communities' Taylor Swift and Karlie Kloss Reunite at Travis Kelce’s Chiefs Game Against Jets

Before “Dil Se..,” Indian cinema’s U.K. and Ireland footprint was largely confined to single screens and community halls in South Asian diaspora hubs such as Southall, Wembley, Leicester and Birmingham. While films like “Hum Aapke Hain Koun..!” (1994) and “Dilwale Dulhania Le Jayenge” (1995) generated significant receipts – eventually crossing the £1 million ($1.6 million) mark over prolonged runs – they operated in an isolated distribution ecosystem outside mainstream computerized reporting.

The breakthrough of “Dil Se..” transformed the market landscape. Just two months later, Karan Johar’s directorial debut “Kuch Kuch Hota Hai” built on that foundation, bowing inside the top 10 and amassing £1.7 million ($3 million). In 2001, Johar’s multi-starrer “Kabhi Khushi Kabhie Gham…” rewrote the record books, reaching No. 3 with £473,255 ($681,000) in its first week from just 41 screens and finishing its run with nearly £2.5 million ($3.6 million). Over the next decade, a string of major Bollywood releases – including Sanjay Leela Bhansali’s “Devdas” (2002), Yash Chopra’s “Veer-Zaara” (2004) and Johar’s “Kabhi Alvida Naa Kehna” (2006) – cemented Hindi-language cinema as a reliable weekend fixture in the U.K. top 10.

The threshold moved higher in 2010 when 20th Century Fox released Karan Johar’s “My Name Is Khan,” starring Shah Rukh Khan and Kajol, on 91 screens. The drama debuted at No. 6 with £936,000 ($1.4 million) and concluded with £2.6 million ($4.1 million). Three years later, Aamir Khan’s action franchise entry “Dhoom 3” (2013) raised the bar further, bowing with £884,567 ($1.4 million) and becoming the first Indian film to cross £2.7 million ($4.4 million) in the territory. Aamir Khan continued to push commercial frontiers with “PK” (2014), which grossed £2.3 million ($3.7 million), and “Dangal” (2016), which delivered £2.5 million ($3.2 million), alongside Salman Khan’s “Bajrangi Bhaijaan” (2015), which reached £2.6 million ($4.1 million).

Yet for all of Bollywood’s commercial muscle, an unyielding glass ceiling remained: chart positions consistently hovered between No. 3 and No. 10 against Hollywood tentpoles. Furthermore, South Indian cinema remained hindered by fragmented distribution.

That began to shift in April 2017 with S.S. Rajamouli’s “Baahubali 2: The Conclusion,” starring Prabhas. The film released across several languages and crossed £1 million ($1.3 million) in the territory within 10 days. However, fragmented reporting hindered its peak chart placement. The official chart, compiled by Comscore, listed each language version separately: the Hindi-language version opened at No. 6 with £221,452 ($286,000) from 50 sites and the Tamil-language version at No. 9 with £194,522 ($251,000) from 39 sites, while the Malayalam and Telugu-language versions charted lower. Even combined, its roughly £455,000 ($587,000) would have placed it only No. 6, behind “Their Finest” (£630,544; $813,000) and far below the £13.1 million ($16.9 million) bow of “Guardians of the Galaxy Vol. 2.”

The ceiling truly began to crack when Rajamouli’s “RRR” landed in March 2022. Driven by massive event demand, the Telugu epic debuted at No. 2 with £650,204 ($858,000) from 160 locations, including previews, held off from the summit only by Warner Bros.’ “The Batman.”

The subsequent wave of pan-Indian spectacle gradually united reporting streams and brought Southern regional industries into the mainstream multiplex fold. Mani Ratnam’s Tamil historical epic “Ponniyin Selvan: I” (2022) delivered a £745,000 ($834,000) opening weekend across 119 U.K. sites, charting at No. 5 and finishing with £1.2 million ($1.3 million). In March 2024, Blessy’s survival drama “Aadujeevitham – The Goat Life,” starring Prithviraj Sukumaran, set benchmarks for Malayalam cinema, debuting at No. 5 with £556,514 ($702,000) and grossing some £717,000 ($903,000) across its run. That summer, Nag Ashwin’s sci-fi epic “Kalki 2898 AD,” also starring Prabhas, released by Dreamz Entertainment across 450 U.K. sites, opened at No. 3 with £886,366 ($1.1 million) and went on to pass £1.5 million ($1.9 million).

Shah Rukh Khan starrers matched the historic No. 2 benchmark of “RRR” twice in 2023. In January, Siddharth Anand’s spy thriller “Pathaan” opened at No. 2 with a £1.4 million ($1.74 million) three-day weekend (reaching £1.97 million, or $2.44 million, over five days), behind James Cameron’s holdover “Avatar: The Way of Water,” on its way to a then-record U.K. total of £4.38 million ($5.4 million). In September 2023, Atlee’s action thriller “Jawan” mirrored the feat, debuting at No. 2 with £1.35 million ($1.67 million). Ranbir Kapoor’s violent action drama “Animal” (2023) opened lower, at No. 6 with £719,000 ($906,000), but legged out to £2.37 million ($3 million).

In March this year, Aditya Dhar’s spy sequel “Dhurandhar: The Revenge” became the fourth Indian film to open at No. 2, with a then-record £1.6 million ($2.1 million) Friday-to-Sunday weekend, or £2.1 million ($2.8 million) including previews. It went on to overtake “Pathaan” as the territory’s highest-grossing Indian release, with more than £4.4 million ($5.9 million).

South Indian cinema hit its own high-water mark in October this year, when Nelson Dilipkumar’s Tamil-language action sequel “Jailer 2,” starring Rajinikanth, opened at No. 3 with £1.1 million ($1.5 million) from just 228 sites, the highest site average of the weekend. Within 10 days, it had become the highest-grossing Tamil film in the territory with £1.6 million ($2.1 million).

Until this week, No. 2 remained the undisputed ceiling for Indian cinema in the territory.

That boundary fell with “Ramayana: Rise of a Legend.” Directed by Nitesh Tiwari (“Dangal”) and produced by Namit Malhotra’s Prime Focus Studios and DNEG in association with Yash’s Monster Mind Creations, the film is the first of a two-part adaptation of the Hindu epic. Ranbir Kapoor plays Rama, Yash plays the demon king Ravana and Sai Pallavi plays Sita, with Sunny Deol as Hanuman and Ravie Dubey as Lakshmana. The story pits the prince against the vengeful king and ends with Ravana’s abduction of Sita, stopping short of the war between them. Backed by a wide, format-heavy rollout across 364 sites that included premium 3D and large-format screens, the film’s £2.1 million ($2.8 million) debut – a new opening record for an Indian film in the territory – outstripped competing releases, including Disney’s “Wild Horse Nine” (£1.48 million; $1.97 million) at No. 2 and Anime Ltd.’s “Godzilla Minus Zero” (£1.3 million; $1.8 million) at No. 3.

From Mani Ratnam’s political romance sneaking into the lower rungs of the top 10 in 1998, through the trailblazing runs of “Baahubali 2,” “RRR,” “Pathaan” and “The Goat Life,” to a mythological tentpole conquering the weekend chart in 2026, Indian cinema has traveled from diaspora counterprogramming to the top of the U.K. and Ireland chart. However, the full-run numbers still lag well behind. The highest-grossing Indian release in the territory, “Dhurandhar: The Revenge,” has taken just over £4.4 million ($5.9 million), a fraction of what the biggest Hollywood and British releases earn. “Project Hail Mary,” which kept “Dhurandhar” off the top spot in March, passed £15 million ($20 million) in two weekends, while “Avatar: The Way of Water” was above £70 million ($86.8 million) by its seventh. “Ramayana” has broken the ceiling for one weekend. Breaking it over a full run is the next test.

THE•TEAM Forms New Board Following Sale Finalization

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The board's formation follows the finalization of Providence Equity Partners' purchase of the booking agency.

Talent agency THE•TEAM has formed a new board of directors, the company announced Tuesday (Nov. 10), marking the first time it has had a public-facing board.

THE•TEAM, formerly known as Wasserman, was sold by founder and former CEO Casey Wasserman to Providence Equity Partners in July following an outcry after Wasserman’s name surfaced in the Epstein Files. The deal was finalized in October.

Joining the new board are Michael Watts (CEO), Elizabeth Lindsey (global president, brands and properties), Jason Ranne (president, global talent representation), Kevin Shivers (co-president, music), Matthew Vandrau (president, EMEA & APAC), Elena Rochelli (chief financial officer) and Jon Liebman (chairman and CEO, Brillstein Entertainment Partners). Each business leader will continue serving in their roles at the company.

Rounding out the board are Providence Equity Partners’ Davis Noell (senior managing director, co-head of North America), Greg Brown (operating partner), Scott Marimow (managing director) and Ahana Maken (senior vp).

“This is an important moment for our company,” Watts said in a statement. “The members of this board have shown the qualities we value most: embracing our culture, supporting their colleagues and delivering exceptional results for our clients. This group will build on our momentum and help us accelerate growth by focusing on the ways we can best support our clients’ goals and aspirations.”

Noell added, “THE•TEAM has an incredibly talented leadership team and we welcome the expertise and perspective that this board will bring to the business. We’re excited about the growth opportunities ahead and will continue to provide capital and strategic partnership to help expand THE•TEAM’s capabilities across sports, music and entertainment.”

THE•TEAM’s music roster includes Chappell Roan, Coldplay, Ed Sheeran, SZA, Kendrick Lamar and Tyler, The Creator, among others.

Webby Awards Launches IMDB-Like Credits Platform to Provide Verified Recognition for Creators Amid AI Growth (Exclusive)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The long-running initiative celebrating the best of the internet will allow winners, nominees and other honored individuals to claim their work, with Shonda Rhimes and Adam Scott among the founding members.

After celebrating its 30th anniversary earlier this year, the Webby Awards is making it easier for winners, nominees and other honored individuals to make sure they’re properly credited for their work.

The IMDB-like Global Webby Credits Registry allows creators, developers, producers, writers and actors to claim verified profiles documenting their contributions. Though the Webbys have long included these names at the bottom of project pages, the profiles bring together a career’s worth of recognized work regardless of whether the people behind these projects move between different companies. Related Stories Movies Ridley Scott Will Miss Academy's Governors Awards, Accept Honorary Oscar Remotely (Exclusive) Movies 'You Can See Everything' Sweeps Critics Choice Documentary Awards

“The people behind great creative work on the internet have never been harder to find, and they’ve never mattered more,” Webby Group executive director Jesse Feister said in a statement. “The Global Webby Credits Registry puts 30 years of Webby recognition in the hands of the more than 150,000 individuals who earned it, and the thousands who will earn it in the years ahead, giving them verified credentials to build reputation in the AI era.”

As AI becomes a bigger tool in both the discovery of information and the generation of creative work, the Webbys argue these verified credits provide a better way to ensure people are recognized for what they’ve made.

“AI is changing how we discover information, including information about people and their work. Search results are increasingly summarized by AI, and as the technology evolves, we expect more of the ways people discover and evaluate creative professionals to be mediated by AI tools,” Feister tells The Hollywood Reporter. “That makes credible, independent records of people’s work more important than ever. A credit on an award-winning project is a meaningful professional credential, but only if that credit is documented somewhere it can be found and trusted. And as AI makes it easier than ever to produce creative work, having a credible record of what you’ve actually contributed to becomes more important, too.”

A number of Webby-recognized individuals, like Shonda Rhimes and Adam Scott, have already published their own verified profiles as founding members. The launch of the Webbys’ credits platform is also being supported by companies that have won Webbys in the past, including PBS, iHeartMedia and R/GA.

“Behind every great PBS digital experience is a team of talented people bringing our mission to life,” PBS’ vice president, multiplatform marketing and content, Amy Wigler, said in a statement. “The Webby Credits Registry gives those individuals recognition that stays with them throughout their careers. We’re excited to help celebrate their contributions and make their work more visible.”

To claim a profile, eligible individuals can search the Webby Credits database for projects they contributed to, claim the credits associated with their work, verify their identity through LinkedIn and publish their profile. As new work is recognized, the people behind those projects can claim their credits as well.

Pikmin 4 cemented the Switch 2 as the best console for clearing out my backlog

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Both as a matter of personal interest and professional obligation, I try to play as many first-party Nintendo releases as I can. But sometimes even the best ones fall through the cracks.

That's what happened to me when Pikmin 4 launched in 2023. I'd never really been able to vibe with the franchise's fascinating blend of plant-based task management and time-limit stress, so even though the fourth entry garnered rave reviews, I never made proper time for it.

I feel like a big old idiot now because, after playing a few hours of the new Switch 2 upgrade, I can see that Pikmin 4 is kind of incredible, even with a fairly modest set of upgrades for Nintendo's new console. My biggest takeaway from this experience, though, is that there really isn't a better current-gen console for catching up on old games and clearing out your backlog than the Switch 2.

There are two things I want to note right off the bat. First, the official title for this release is Pikmin 4 – Nintendo Switch 2 Edition + Dandori Academy, which is a comically tedious thing to type out more than once, so I won't be doing that. Second, this is just a $10 upgrade for those who already own Pikmin 4 on Switch 1, and the changes this new version brings are not exactly paradigm-shifting.

As with every "Switch 2 Edition" release of an older game, Pikmin 4 has received a boost in resolution. This was already a gorgeous game on Switch 1, with varied and colorful environments, expressive lighting, and a generally warm and welcoming atmosphere, but the Switch 2 version is sharper and pops even more on a nice TV. Unfortunately, the frame rate, which was capped at 30 frames per second on Switch 1, has not been upgraded at all for Switch 2. That's a bummer, but not a deal-breaker.

Players can also use the Switch 2's microphone to bark voice commands at Oatchi, their loyal space dog friend, and this is a feature. I turned that off almost immediately because it's impossible to hold a conversation with someone while playing the game that way. The beefiest addition is Dandori Academy, a mode with a series of new challenge levels. I haven't had time to really dive in yet, but Pikmin 4 veterans looking for something new to do will probably welcome it.

The thing is, aside from the visual boost, I'm not really concerned with what's new in the Switch 2 version of Pikmin 4 because the whole game is new to me. And what a delight it is! Pikmin is all about plucking dozens of cute little plant dudes from the dirt, color-coded according to their unique abilities, and ordering them to do things for you. They can clear obstacles, open up shortcuts, defeat enemies, and most important, carry treasures you find back to your spaceship so you can unlock upgrades and become even more efficient at managing this whole operation.

Strategy games are typically not my cup of tea, but Pikmin scratches a substantial itch for me in that it lets me plant the seeds (literally, in this case) for efficient, automatic processes to play out, and then lets me sit back and observe. It's eminently satisfying to order 10 Pikmin to go open up a shortcut while 10 others defeat an enemy and use the shortcut to carry its corpse back to your ship for harvesting.

And while the decision to give Pikmin 4 no overall time limit might be a detriment to series die-hards, it's a huge boon to me, a guy who stresses out easily. Even after just a few hours, I can see that this is one of the coolest games Nintendo has made in the past five or six years, and the Switch 2 version helped me arrive at that conclusion.

Gaming is an expensive and time-consuming hobby, and it can be nearly impossible for anyone who doesn't review games for a living to play most new releases and participate in the zeitgeist around them. What I've come to realize since the Switch 2 came out, though, is that playing older games all the time instead of feverishly speeding through every new release actually kicks ass, and for my money, this is the best current console platform to do that on.

Nothing will ever beat a PC in this regard, but if you don't have one of those, the Switch 2 is your best bet right now. The PS5's ability to play games from before the PS4 generation is limited at best, and while the Xbox Series consoles can play some games from every Xbox generation, Microsoft has more or less stopped adding new old games to the console backwards compatibility list.

Meanwhile, the Switch 2 can play most Switch 1 games, including countless re-releases, remakes, and remasters of games from across all of gaming history. You can play (versions of) almost every mainline Final Fantasy game on Switch 2. You can play many Resident Evil games on Switch 2, and in fact, that's how I finally learned to love Resident Evil 4 earlier this year. Thanks to Capcom's preservation efforts, I can also play basically every Mega Man game on this machine.

That's before you even get into more obscure stuff that's been preserved on Switch, like the Arcade Archives collection, or quality modern ports of weirdo games like Tail of the Sun and Oddworld: Stranger's Wrath. There's just so much to dig into.

A lot of the above is also true of PS5 and Xbox, but those machines lack decades of first-party Nintendo games that exist either as native Switch 1/2 releases, or as part of the Nintendo Classics collection. With Nintendo Switch Online, I can turn on my Switch 2 right now and play dozens of NES, SNES, and Game Boy games. The Expansion Pack adds N64, Genesis, Game Boy Advance, and GameCube games to the mix, and I've greatly enjoyed digging through those libraries since the Switch 2 came out.

All of that, combined with Nintendo's "Switch 2 Edition" releases, which give some Switch 1 games a sorely needed upgrade, has given me my old games mojo back after it went missing for a few years. I realize it sounds strange to recommend a $500 machine on the basis that it's great at playing old games, but, well, here we are. Adobe Firefly AI Assistant can now take on recurring tasks for you

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The company is also bringing an agent to the Premiere mobile app.

Few companies have embraced artificial intelligence quite like Adobe. Between Photoshop, Premiere and Illustrator, the company has brought its Firefly assistant, and other generative AI features, to much of its Creative Cloud suite. Adobe is showing no signs of slowing down. At its Max conference in Miami today, the company announced a significant expansion to Creative Cloud's agentic capabilities.

To start, taking a page from Anthropic and OpenAI, Adobe is dipping its toes into the world of proactive agents. An updated version of its Firefly AI Assistant can now carry out multi-step tasks. When you give the AI Assistant a job, a new planning mode allows you to review and edit the agent's proposed workflow before giving it approval to move forward. You can also select a specific model to orchestrate the tasks or leave that decision up to Adobe's software. At the same, the company is giving users more fine control over a few different customization options. For example, you can now define colors and brand guidelines that will apply across projects the assistant takes on for you.

In addition to making AI Assistant more capable, Adobe is also previewing new always-on agents that can take on recurring tasks for you. In a preview the company shared with Engadget, it demoed a user prompting Firefly AI Assistant to sort through footage as it was being uploaded to a project. The assistant offered to organize the raw files, automatically apply LUTs and clean up audio, as well as back up the files, which the user approved in the new planning mode. After organizing the footage, the user tasked AI Assistant with preparing a social media promotion campaign for the project.

Separately, Adobe is bringing a new agent to the Premiere mobile app, which first debuted on iOS last year before later arriving on Android. As you can imagine, the agent is designed to help users make edits using natural language. Adobe is first making this tool available in private preview before rolling it out more broadly. Lastly, the company is testing a new set of MCP connections that allow you to give your favorite chatbot local access to Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere and After Effects so that it can do things like edit layers for you.

As Adobe took me through many of its Max announcements, the one thing I kept thinking about was if the company's end goal was to eventually bypass the creatives that have been its customers for so long. After all, the number of people who might be skilled with Photoshop and Premiere is far smaller than the group might want to use its capabilities.

To point, in one demo, Zorana Gee, the company's senior director of agentic product, mentioned how entrepreneurs could use Firefly AI Assistant's capabilities to create social posts for their small business. "Ultimately, we're a creative company serving creatives," she said when I asked her about that question. "Depending on where you are on that continuum is where we will meet you, but ultimately it's about how do we allow anybody to hone their craft."

I also asked Gee about Artcraft, the free Adobe alternative that was made possible by the growing sophistication of coding models from Anthropic and OpenAI. Gee said she felt the "professionalism, security and reliability" of Adobe's products would be what allows the company to keep users invested in its ecosystem.

Spotify adds new voices for its AI DJ

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

You can also now listen to a DJ mix without the commentary.

Spotify is adding more ways to customize the experience of using its AI-generated DJ. The feature began rolling out back in 2023 as a way to get a radio-like, lean-back listening experience tailored to each listener's individual tastes with an AI voiceover offering context or chatter about the selections. DJ has since expanded to taking requests and supporting additional languages.

Listeners can now also choose a personality for the voice talking them through the song selections. Spotify is rolling out seven personas in English and two in Spanish. Each one has a name and a different approach, ranging from "laid back" to "gregarious." The English language character lineup has three US voices, one "UK," one Irish, one Australian and one Indian. Spanish listeners will have two Mexican voices to choose between.

Spotify has also added an option for turning off the DJ commentary, which means the AI voice will not offer any chatter between songs. It will only speak to introduce the listening session or when it's directed to change up the music choices. The company says it's "perfect for moments like hosting a dinner party."

Photoshop, Lightroom, and Premiere are making it easier to remove things

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Here are the new editing and design tools coming to Creative Cloud apps and Adobe’s Firefly suite.﻿

Here are the new editing and design tools coming to Creative Cloud apps and Adobe’s Firefly suite.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Adobe is expanding its Creative Cloud apps and Firefly AI platform with new capabilities that aim to make design and editing projects faster and less complicated. Some of the announcements from Adobe’s Max event today include features for automatically removing objects, blemishes, and other unwanted distractions in Premiere, Photoshop, and Lightroom, alongside new generative AI tools and collaborative experiences across Adobe’s apps and Firefly suite.

In Premiere, Adobe is introducing a new Generative Remove tool in beta that lets you erase unwanted objects and distractions like boom mics and background clutter directly on the video editing timeline. Users can mask and track an object, click Remove, and then Premiere “fills the area with realistic detail” using generative AI, according to Adobe, while the rest of the footage surrounding the erased area remains untouched. We saw a similar capability called Project Frame Forward demonstrated in last year’s Sneaks showcase, which is what Adobe calls the experimental features it’s developing.

Premiere is also getting new sound editing tools that help to quickly clean up messy recordings and separate various audio sources — including dialogue, music, ambience, and sound effects — into individual tracks and elements. The Generative Media tool Adobe introduced earlier this year is now generally available, alongside the new Color Mode color editing experience that Adobe launched in beta in April.

Photoshop now has an updated Remove Tool, which is better at detecting and reconstructing surrounding elements associated with the objects it’s erasing, such as reflections, shadows, and footprints. Features that are new to Photoshop include Quick Compare, which gives you a before-and-after canvas split-screen slider to review changes and edits without jumping between layers, and Relight, which adds new light sources that you can position around the canvas to dynamically adjust the lighting of images, with support for customizing hue and brightness.

The blemish removal tool in Lightroom on mobile is now coming to the desktop version, allowing you to retouch skin in photographs with a single tap. Open Eyes is also coming to Lightroom in public beta, which fixes blinking mishaps by replacing closed eyes with “natural-looking” open eyed results by matching a reference photo of the subject from your image library.

Adobe’s Firefly AI creative suite is getting new tools for video and image editing, and Quick Actions — ready-to-use workflows that specialize in automating specific tasks, such as making video thumbnails and product ads — are now generally available. Some new beta experiences are also launching in Firefly, including the Firefly Design combined edifying space for illustration, image editing, animation, 3D, and print. There’s also Firefly Graph, which “helps creators build and iterate on custom, repeatable workflows,” according to Adobe, and looks rather like the Weave platform that Figma introduced last year. And in private beta, Adobe introduced a Firefly Builder tool that’s designed around prototyping websites and apps — which also shares some similarities with Figma Make.

We’ve called out the most interesting announcements from Adobe Max here, but it’s just a fraction of the updates being showcased at the event. You can find the full list of all the new AI and design features announced today on Adobe’s blog.

Adobe is going all in on agentic AI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Third-party AI agents can use Creative Cloud apps for you, and Firefly’s AI Assistant can run your social media accounts.

Third-party AI agents can use Creative Cloud apps for you, and Firefly’s AI Assistant can run your social media accounts.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Adobe is committing itself to the notion that chatbots and AI agents could be your next design assistant, or even automate creative workflows and repetitive tasks on your behalf. Some of the tools and features announced today at Adobe’s Max conference in Miami, Florida, include allowing your preferred third-party AI agents to access and change Creative Cloud projects, new capabilities for Adobe’s Firefly AI Assistant, and the ability for AI agents to keep perpetually running recurring work in the background.

Local Model Context Protocol (MCP) integrations for Creative Cloud tools were previewed at the event, which aim to give you a choice between using Adobe’s own AI assistants, or the third-party agents you already use everyday. The integrations enable external AI agents from providers like OpenAI, Google, and Anthropic to work locally in Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere, and After Effects, where they can make direct changes to native project files on your computer.

You can take over from the agents at any time to manually refine the work because all layers, vectors, bins, and sequences remain editable. Agents that are connected to other MCP-capable tools can also include them in the same workflow, such as asking the AI agent to pull reference documents from Google Docs, design a project around them in Adobe’s tools, and then share the results with colleagues or clients in Slack. Creative Cloud local MCP integrations aren’t available yet, but Adobe says a private beta will be launching in early 2027.

“Always-on” agents were also previewed at Adobe’s Max event, which can perform recurring tasks continuously in the background, and allow you to focus on other work or step away from your computer entirely. You can assign them a job, such as organizing incoming assets, ongoing quality checks, or preparing content, and then review the agent’s plan and approve the final outcome. For example, an agent could be assigned to automatically turn all incoming images into promotional graphics for different social media channels, allowing you to check in daily and select the best results to publish. Adobe hasn’t specified when this preview will launch, but says it’ll be coming to Firefly AI Assistant first.

The Firefly AI Assistant beta, which is powered by Adobe’s creative agent, is also getting some upgrades. New capabilities include a Plan Mode that enables users to review, edit, and approve the agent’s plan before it starts running a task, a Model Selector that lets you specify which AI model to use for a project or leave it on auto-select to automatically assign the model based on the required results, and a new integration with Firefly Boards. Firefly AI Assistant now also lets you customize preferred formats, colors, brand guidelines, characters, and products, and choose to automatically apply those preferences across every project.

The creative agent has been used to stick AI assistants into Premiere, Photoshop, Illustrator, InDesign, After Effects, and Frame.io since it was introduced earlier this year, and now it’s expanding further into video editing. In Premiere, it can now be used to track masked subjects through a frame and apply effects or adjust color and exposure across entire clip sequences. The agent is also being added to Premiere mobile editing app, allowing you to make conversational video edits while on the go.

If it feels like Adobe is slapping agents and chatbots into all of its products then you’d be correct. That’s all according to the plan the company’s creativity head David Wadhwani laid out in April when he said the Adobe creative agent will eventually surface “in virtually everything we develop.” Whether it’s developing its own agentic solutions or throwing open the doors to third-party bots, AI agents will play a key role in Adobe’s future.

Spotify’s AI DJ gets new voices, personalities, and an option for less chatter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Spotify’s AI DJ, one of the company’s earlier efforts in embracing AI technology to connect people with their favorite music, is getting an upgrade. On Tuesday, the streamer announced that users will now be able to customize their DJ more like they can an AI assistant, by choosing between different voices and personalities. Those who like the feature, but not all the chatter, can also now opt for an experience with less commentary in between sets.

Since first launching the AI DJ in 2023, Spotify has leveraged AI across to improve all areas of its app. Most recently, that has included updates that allow users to chat with a conversational AI agent about music, fine-tune their recommendations with prompts, create playlists with AI, and work with third-party agents like ChatGPT and Claude. However, the DJ remains one of Spotify’s most popular AI features, as it’s used by over 94M Spotify Premium listeners.

Now, it’s revisiting one of its first major AI features, taking a cue from the personalization options other AI assistants routinely offer.

The different voices will have their own unique personalities as well. For instance, users can pick between three American voices, one of which is laid-back and warm (Casey), another that’s direct and has an edge (Tess), and a third that has a Southern accent and a youthful energy (Lane). In addition to the other various regional accents, there are also two new Spanish voices, one that’s more down-to-earth (Nico) and one that’s more witty and friendly (Fabi).

The voices will not replace the AI DJ’s original voice, “X,” which was based on that of a Spotify employee’s real voice recordings. However, DJ Fabi and Nico will now be the only Spanish voices available, replacing the earlier “Livi.”

The other change allows users to toggle off the commentary between songs, reducing the DJ’s interruptions. While typically, the AI DJ introduces different selections of music, explaining what it picked and why, users can now limit it to brief acknowledgements when they first start the DJ feature and when they turn it off. Spotify says this would allow people to use the DJ at times they may have turned to a playlist instead, like when hosting a party where they want music in the background, but not the chatter.

To access the DJ, users can search for the term “DJ” in the search bar of the Spotify app on their device, then tap the three-dot menu to pick their voice and commentary settings. To change genres, artists, or recommendations while the DJ is driving things, you simply tap the DJ button again.

Spotify says the new voices begin rolling out today, November 10, to Premium users in the markets where DJ is already supported. Currently, that includes over 95 global markets, where the DJ is supported in English, Spanish, French, German, Italian, and Brazilian Portuguese.

Peak Design’s Roller Lite Carry-On is bigger on the inside — and cheaper, too

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

More efficient and more affordable, but less charming, too.﻿

More efficient and more affordable, but less charming, too.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When we sampled Peak Design’s Roller Pro last year, we loved almost everything about the camera gear powerhouse’s first attempt at a proper suitcase. Everything, that is, except for the price. Back then, during its crowdfunded launch, the Pro could be had for as little as $425. Today, you’re looking at $599.95 for the same roller bag.

Peak Design has taken that feedback to heart in creating the Roller Lite Carry-On, part of a new series of suitcases that bring many of the Pro’s features and design concepts into a hard-sided clamshell design that’s more traditional and, crucially, more affordable, starting with the $449.95 Lite Carry-On.

It’s part of a new Lite family of suitcases. The slightly larger Lite Carry-On Plus costs $469.99 and measures 22.5 x 15 x 9 inches, while the $549.95 Lite Trunk is 30 x 16.1 x 13.8 inches. The former should still squeeze in most overhead compartments, while the latter is better off checked. Both, though, come in cheaper than the Pro.

That cost-cutting does result in bags with fewer features and less originality, but I still found the new Lite Carry-On to be a great travel companion, first for a two-day whirlwind journey to Rome, then for a week in Tucson, Arizona.

I travel a lot, spending far more time escorting my carry-on around airports than I do driving my car at home, so no surprise that I’m picky about what I drag through terminals and haul up into overhead compartments. I wouldn’t say that aesthetics are a primary concern, but everybody prefers a good-looking suitcase, and the Roller Lite passes that first test. It’s understated yet sophisticated in black, but the emerald green option called cypress might be even better. There’s also a hue called stone for those more into earth tones.

Regardless of color, I think the Lite Carry-On’s hard plastic shell looks better than the soft-sided Roller Pro, and I’d hazard a guess it’ll stand the test of time better, too. The second trip I took with the Lite started in a tiny puddle-jumper of a plane. The flight attendant gate-checked any bag with wheels, even though the Lite Carry-On (21.7 x 13.8 x 9 inches, slightly smaller than the Pro) would have easily fit in the overhead.

It came back to me from its trip to the belly of the aircraft with a few new scuffs and scratches, otherwise looking only a little worse for wear.

Durability was a big selling point on the Roller Pro, and that continues here. The Lite is even more repairable thanks to its easily replaced strap handles. They sit flush without taking up any room in the suitcase itself, yet are quite comfortable to use.

Both the Pro and Lite share the same carbon fiber handle, also easily replaceable. It’s my favorite feature, rising smoothly and dropping into place without a hitch. I especially love the dry carbon weave that’s exposed when it’s raised.

My only complaint is that the handle on top is too bulky, making it a clumsy fit through the luggage passthrough on my backpack. But otherwise, the carbon construction makes for a sturdier feel than the handle on my Tumi 19 Degree Aluminum roller, which costs three times as much.

While both share the same handle, the Lite’s design is a substantial departure from the Pro. The Pro offered a so-called “hybrid” layout, with the top opening up and swinging back into a single large compartment. The Lite is a more traditional clamshell with a zipper running the full circumference, splitting the case in two like a book.

When it comes to packing camera equipment and other gear, I think the Pro’s single main compartment is easier to live with, especially if you’re hauling some bulky telephoto glass.

But when I’m packing more items of clothing than camera gear, I actually prefer the Lite Carry-On’s two smaller compartments. I can separate clothes from everything else, which is useful when living out of a suitcase.

The Lite Carry-On lacks the Pro’s trick external pockets for laptops, passports, and the like. That doesn’t bother me, as that stuff always goes in my backpack, but if you’re looking for a single-bag travel experience, the Pro is better suited. The Lite is also missing the Cord Hook feature of the Pro, potentially useful for strapping a tripod or the like to the outside.

Inside, the right half of the Lite has a zippered flap cover with two large mesh pockets that don’t look or feel as good as the inner pockets on the Pro. You’ll still find a hidden pocket for tucking away an Apple AirTag tracker, though.

The other side has the same elastic-strap holding system as the Pro. You load it up with stuff, then stretch the two hooks across to latch on the other side. It’s a basic, flimsy-looking solution, but it works. When the agents at Leonardo da Vinci International Airport wanted to rummage around in my bag, those little bungee hooks kept everything from scattering over the floor in Fiumicino.

The Lite Carry-On has an integrated, TSA-approved lock, something missing on the Pro, and though it’s slightly smaller on the outside (21.7 x 13.8 vs. 21.8 x 14), removing that front pocket makes it substantially bigger on the inside: 38 liters vs. 34 on the Pro, expanding to 45 liters vs. 39.

The 2-inch expansion on the Lite is a great emergency solution if you picked up a few too many souvenirs in Seville, but the Lite feels loose and floppy when rolling around with its expansion zipper open. I don’t recommend it.

Otherwise, when fully zipped and packed, I really enjoyed wheeling the Roller Lite around. It was an easy glider over smooth terminal floors and didn’t rattle obnoxiously over rougher asphalt.

The Roller Lite Carry-On is undoubtedly a simpler suitcase than the Roller Pro that preceded it, and because of that, it doesn’t feel nearly as special to use or purpose-driven as Peak Design’s offerings typically are.

But then it’s $150 cheaper, roomier, is easier to pack, and, in my estimation, will prove even more durable. It may not be the ultimate roller bag for pro photogs, but it’s my new favorite suitcase for under $500. I’d even put it ahead of options from Tumi, Briggs & Riley, and others that cost many times more, aren’t as easily repaired, and don’t come with a lifetime warranty.

After challenging Google’s Android with Cyanogen, its co-founder takes aim at Windows for AI era

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Almost a decade after Cyanogen shut down its Android operating-system business following a years-long, unsuccessful effort to loosen Google’s dominance, its co-founder, Kirt McMaster, is now targeting Microsoft’s Windows with a new operating system designed for people who rely heavily on artificial intelligence in their everyday work.

McMaster’s company, Kora Systems, on Tuesday unveiled a Linux-based desktop operating system that integrates AI models and autonomous agents directly into the computing experience, allowing them to work across applications, access files, and retain context between tasks. The software is expected to launch in 2027 as a free-to-use desktop operating system, with support for existing Intel- and AMD-powered PCs as well as machines running Qualcomm’s Snapdragon chips.

Kora is developing the eponymous operating system with a team of about 50 engineers, relying on AI coding tools including OpenAI’s Codex and Anthropic’s Claude. McMaster told TechCrunch that these tools have accelerated development by at least fivefold, with most of the gains coming over the past six months.

Microsoft has long dominated the PC operating-system market, making it hard for newer players to establish a foothold. Windows held about 77% of the worldwide desktop operating-system market in October, compared with nearly 10% for Apple’s OS X, according to StatCounter’s web traffic data.

However, McMaster believes Kora does not need to compete for Windows’ entire user base. He is rather targeting people who use AI extensively and are willing to pay for access to powerful models and services.

“Microsoft can keep its billion users,” McMaster said, estimating that 45 million to 50 million people currently account for much of AI usage and revenue. He expects that market to exceed 200 million users by 2033.

McMaster told TechCrunch that his experience at Cyanogen, where he spent over five years, taught him the dangers of relying on the very company he was trying to challenge. Cyanogen remained heavily dependent on Google Mobile Services despite offering features beyond Google’s version of Android. That ultimately restricted its ability to build an independent ecosystem.

“If you are trying to compete with an incumbent but are still reliant on them, you have no chance,” he said.

Kora, McMaster said, builds on an idea he pursued toward the end of his time at Cyanogen, when he began exploring ways to integrate third-party services directly into the operating system. With Kora, he wants AI models and agents to work across applications and access files without being confined to individual apps.

Previously known as Playtron, a gaming operating-system company led by McMaster, the Palo Alto-based company rebranded as Kora in July 2025 as it shifted its focus to AI. The decision came as early companies including Perplexity and OpenAI were moving into AI-powered web browsers, McMaster said. He believed browsers would not give AI agents enough access to a computer to carry out more complex tasks, making an operating system a better starting point.

The shift also allowed Kora to build on nearly three years of work developing Playtron’s Linux-based gaming operating system, called GameOS, instead of starting from scratch.

Kora is designed to let users work with different AI models and agents across applications, while keeping track of conversations, documents, and other information. It also allows AI agents to operate in separate workspaces with restricted access to files and other resources.

While McMaster has drawn on his experience at Cyanogen, none of Kora’s engineers worked there. Instead, the company recruited heavily from the Linux gaming community, he told TechCrunch.

Kora’s software is built on Linux and uses Nix. It replaces the Fedora Silverblue foundation of Playtron’s earlier GameOS.

While Linux and Nix are open source, McMaster said Kora’s own technology, including its Cortex AI engine and desktop interface, remains proprietary. The company, he said, intends to support AI models and services from different providers, although it has not committed to making its own software open source.

Gaming also remains part of Kora’s plans. McMaster said the company intends to introduce AI-powered tools that would allow developers to create, publish, and play games directly within the operating system. He added that support for Android apps is also on its roadmap.

McMaster told TechCrunch that Kora’s team has worked closely with Qualcomm’s engineers for nearly a year to optimize the operating system specifically for Snapdragon-powered PCs. The collaboration includes support for Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X2 Elite chips.

Alongside Qualcomm, Kora is working with Saudi Arabia’s AI company Humain on an enterprise version of the operating system, called Humain OS, which is expected to run on Snapdragon-powered laptops in 2027. Kora also plans to announce partnerships with PC manufacturers at a later date, though McMaster did not identify any companies or provide a specific timeline.

Kora plans to generate revenue through subscriptions that bundle access to AI models and agentic services. Moreover, its Cortex engine is designed to run AI tasks locally when possible and use cloud-based models for work requiring more computing power.

Kora’s investors include Samsung Next, Square Enix, Alumni Ventures, Polychain, VanEck, and Circle, all of which previously backed Playtron. The company, McMaster said, has raised additional funding since shifting its focus to AI. However, he declined to disclose the total amount raised or its valuation. Kora has enough funding to operate for several years, he said.

Aura’s next color E Ink photo frame is smaller, cheaper, sharper, and more colorful

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The new 10-inch version of the Aura Ink can display three new colors that help skin tones look more natural.﻿

The new 10-inch version of the Aura Ink can display three new colors that help skin tones look more natural.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Aura just launched a smaller version of its color E Ink frame with a handful of improvements and a more affordable price. The new Aura Ink frame features a 10-inch E Ink panel that can display more colors at a higher pixel density. It’s also differentiated from the original 13-inch model with a black frame and matching mat board that helps photos stand out.

The Aura Ink 10 is now available in the US for $349, $150 cheaper than the 13-inch version, which still costs $499. The original Ink’s price made it a tough sell over Aura’s other frames that are available with an LCD screen as large as 15 inches for $299. The new 10-inch option makes color E Ink more accessible, and it carries forward one of the Ink’s biggest advantages: a rechargeable battery so the frame can hang on a wall without a dangling power cord.

Although color E Ink displays have been available in e-readers like Kindles and Kobos for a few years, those devices use a color filter over a standard black-and-white e-paper panel with limited color reproduction. The Aura Ink instead uses E Ink’s Spectra 6 technology, which creates patterns of black, white, red, yellow, blue, and green ink droplets to reproduce a much wider color spectrum.

For the new 10-inch version, Aura worked with E Ink to add three custom base colors created by mixing those six inks in new ways: off-white, mustard yellow, and orange. The goal was to improve the company’s proprietary dithering algorithm to produce more natural-looking colors on faces, add detail to textures like grass, and to help smooth gradients so photos of sunsets, for example, look better. The 10-inch panel also has a higher pixel density — 200ppi, up from 150ppi on the 13-inch model — allowing it to produce slightly sharper results.

Both are welcome improvements, but in practice the color reproduction isn’t drastically better than the original. You’d need to compare the same photo on both models side by side to really see a difference, and the upgrades don’t overcome one of the major shortcomings of the Spectra 6 technology. While E Ink estimates it can reproduce around 65,000 different colors — and potentially even more with Aura’s enhancements — LCD displays can produce millions.

That can make it hard to predict how your photos will look on the new Aura Ink. Rich colors — like blue sky, green trees, and bright red flags — really pop, but skin tones can still sometimes look too warm or cold. I sent to the frame a photo of a bright-yellow water taxi photographed on a sunny day and expected it to appear well saturated, but that particular shade of yellow came out disappointingly subdued.

The technology still has its challenges, but the benefits of E Ink’s color displays make the new Aura Ink a tempting alternative to the company’s LCD-based frames, particularly for those who don’t want another glowing screen at home. Photos displayed on the Aura Ink look like a printed photo, even with a subtle front light that automatically adjusts to boost the lighting in a room.

It would fit right in on a wall of printed photos, and Aura estimates you’d need to take it down every three months to charge. But that’s if you update the displayed photo once every night. You can increase the frequency through Aura’s mobile app to up to 12 automatic refreshes per day, or manually change it using a set of buttons hidden on the back if you don’t mind charging the Ink more often. Just be mindful that unlike an e-reader that refreshes a page of text in less than a second, it takes the Ink about 30 seconds to change to a new image after a series of flickering, psychedelic patterns.

I’ve been testing it as a standalone picture frame using a removable support that props the Aura Ink up in landscape or horizontal orientations. Moving it around the house without hunting for an outlet first is a big plus. And as with all of Aura’s digital photo frames, the new 10-inch Ink also benefits from the polished mobile app that has helped make the company’s products so popular. You can send photos to the Ink through a Wi-Fi connection with free, unlimited cloud storage or create shared albums that friends and family can view and contribute to. It also works with Google Photos and Apple Photos. If you don’t have access to the app, you can text or email photos directly to the frame.

If you plan to gift an Aura frame during the holidays, the company’s LCD models are still more budget-friendly, with 10-inch options starting at $149. The new version of the Aura Ink still feels like a device for early adopters who completely understand the tradeoffs of E Ink’s color screens. But if you prefer the look of printed photos over a glowing LCD, the smaller Aura Ink is now one of the most affordable ways to try out this tech.
