ABC Is Failing ‘High Potential’ and ‘The Rookie’ Once Again

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

ABC's fall procedural lineup has just two weeks left until it airs its mid-season finales and goes on hiatus for the winter, but the premiere dates for the spring lineup have only just been announced. Like 9-1-1, 9-1-1: Nashville, Grey's Anatomy, and R.J. Decker, ABC's lineup of procedurals premiering their new seasons this January all concluded their most recent seasons last spring. Whereas fans of the aforementioned shows only had to wait for the length of the standard summer hiatus, though, fans of the second group of shows will have had to wait up to nine months by the time they return.

For a show like Will Trent, which has been on this timeline since its debut, this is to be expected. The Rookie and High Potential, on the other hand, initially got to air in both the fall and the spring, and have since been relegated to just the latter. For two of ABC's most popular shows, it's already disappointing that they only get to air for a few months of the year. Now, though, there are just under two months until these shows return the first full week of January, and it's frustrating that their return dates have not been announced until last week.

The Rookie has been on the spring release schedule for three seasons now, but High Potential has only just been moved to this lineup. It's a particularly jarring choice for High Potential, considering the series is only on its third season, and the show's second season had monumental records of success. Just as the series was really gaining momentum, it was put on break for nine months, leaving fans to speculate on their own about the fallout of the Season 2 finale's intense cliffhanger ending. Fans of The Rookie, meanwhile, may be used to this by now, but it is still frustrating to watch a beloved Network TV show only air for about a third of the year, then go on break for the rest of it.

The Rookie started as a show about a man starting over at forty-five and turned into a proper ensemble drama somewhere around season three. Here is which of them you are, and the case for yours being the best of the four.

You started late and you are not embarrassed about it. Nolan walks into the academy at forty-five surrounded by people half his age, and the show is honest that this is both his biggest disadvantage and the only reason he is any good at the job.

You are better at this than people expect and you intend to keep proving it. Chen goes from rookie to the best undercover officer in the division, which works because she is genuinely good at becoming somebody else and genuinely bad at pretending she does not care.

You are hard on people because you have decided that is what caring looks like. Bradford is introduced as the worst training officer in the building and slowly revealed as the one who takes the job most seriously, which are the same fact seen from two angles.

You said what you wanted and then you went and got it. Lopez wanted detective from the first episode, said so out loud, and got there by being better at the work than the people who were quieter about wanting it.

There are a lot of benefits to being a fan of a Network TV show, from getting to watch the episodes live alongside other fans to getting to speculate each week about what will happen next. The best part, though, is loving a show with a predictable release schedule. When series then start changing their release schedule to only air for half the time, it is incredibly disappointing to fans. It means potentially fewer episodes for the new season, as well as a much longer wait in between seasons, dampening some of the excitement and anticipation earned by the finales of the previous seasons.

In a perfect world, ABC would move High Potential and The Rookie back to its fall release schedule, but as it stands, the network just needs to do better by the procedurals in its spring lineup. Both of these shows excellently set up suspense ahead of their new seasons by ending their most recent ones with massive cliffhangers after two explosive episodes. For The Rookie, the fan-favorite pairing of Lucy Chen (Melissa O'Neil) and Tim Bradford (Eric Winter) finally got engaged in a beautiful beach proposal, only to both get abducted seconds later. High Potential, on the other hand, introduced another new twist in the ongoing mystery of what happened to Roman Sinquerra. After Captain Nick Wagner (Steve Howey) went to a meetup to get more information on this mystery, Morgan Gillory (Kaitlin Olson) found him shot and left for dead, leaving Wagner's fate up in the air over the hiatus.

When both of these series concluded their most recent seasons, fans were left champing at the bit to find out what happens next. While there are still devoted fans excited for these series to return, there has no doubt been a loss of momentum for both of these series after their unusually long break. Most fans will likely have to do a refresher on the events of the previous seasons to remember the main events ahead of their returns, and while that's become standard for shows on streaming now, this is still atypical of Network TV shows. ABC needs to do better with the shows in its spring lineup, and to stop moving shows to mid-season after they've previously been airing for the entire season.

High Potential returns to ABC on Tuesday, January 5, at 9:00 P.M. EST. The Rookie returns to ABC the following night, on Wednesday, January 6, at 9:00 P.M. EST.

US CPI Released: October Inflation Beats Expectations

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

U.S. inflation came in hotter than economists expected in October, with headline CPI rising 0.4% month-on-month, above the 0.3% forecast. Core CPI also increased 0.3%, beating the 0.2% expectation.

This has pressured markets, with Bitcoin falling below $73,500 as the odds of a Fed rate hike surged.

The latest CPI data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics showed headline inflation rising 0.4% in October, compared with economists’ expectations for a 0.3% increase.

Core CPI, which excludes food and energy prices, rose 0.3% month-on-month, above the 0.2% forecast. The figure matched the monthly increase recorded in September.

Meanwhile, the BLS has not released an official year-over-year CPI rate for October because the 43-day government shutdown in 2025 left it without the October 2025 data needed to make the annual comparison.

The stronger CPI reading quickly shifted attention to the Federal Reserve’s next policy decision. After the data release, the CME FedWatch Tool showed the probability of a 25-basis-point rate hike rising from 84% to 99%.

Speaking at the Bank of England Watchers Conference, Fed Governor Christopher Waller said he would support raising interest rates by 25 basis points to 4%–4.25% in December.

He also warned that if core inflation rose above 0.3%, the Fed could consider a larger 50-basis-point hike.

The hotter reading strengthened the case for tighter monetary policy and increased attention on the Fed’s December decision.

In response to the CPI data release, Bitcoin’s price saw a slight drop of 1%, reaching $73,479, with its market cap falling to $1.48 trillion.

The broader cryptocurrency market also came under selling pressure as traders reassessed the outlook for U.S. interest rates.

Hoping to secure promotion back to League B, Montenegro welcome strugglers Latvia to Stadion Pod Goricom for a UEFA Nations League C clash on Thursday night.

Hrabri Sokoli enjoyed an unbeaten start to the current cycle during the September/October international window, whereas 11 Vilki are still searching for their maiden success of the competition.

After finishing only above European minnows Gibraltar in World Cup qualifying last year, Montenegro have experienced a much-needed upturn in results during this season's Nations League C campaign.

Hrabri Sokoli strung together an impressive four-game unbeaten streak (W3 D1) during the September/October international period, with three consecutive wins followed by a goalless draw with Armenia in Podgorica on October 5.

Collecting a commendable 10 points from their opening quartet of fixtures, Mirko Vucinic's side are sitting pretty at the summit of the League C2 standings, guaranteed of a top-two finish in the section.

A victory over whipping boys Latvia on Thursday night could seal Montenegro top spot in the group and automatic promotion with a game to spare, given that Cyprus drop points earlier in the day.

Aiming to help his side extend their unbeaten run in all international contests past six matches (W4 D2), Sassuolo midfielder Vasilije Adzic is a rising star for Montenegro, scoring twice in the 2-1 victory over Latvia in October.

Latvia avoided relegation to League D of the Nations League with a two-legged success over Gibraltar in March, however, the Baltic nation are struggling to prove that they are worthy of a spot in their current group.

A wretched September/October window ended in three defeats and a single draw for 11 Vilki, who have managed just two competitive victories since a 1-0 triumph at minnows Andorra in March 2025.

With only a point to their name as the Nations League group stage draws to a close, Latvia are rooted to the bottom of the League C2 table, six points behind Cyprus in the promotion-playoff position.

In truth, 11 Vilki are yet to see any tangible on-field improvements under the stewardship of 59-year-old Paolo Nicolato, whose experience with the Italian youth teams was meant to bring new ideas to the Stadions Skonto side.

Latvia have faced off against Montenegro on five occasions over the past 14 years, with Hrabri Sokoli unbeaten (W3 D2), although the Baltic nation have drawn their past two matches at Stadion Pod Goricom.

Picking up a red card during a cameo against Armenia last time out, Montenegro midfielder Stefan Lonar is suspended for Thursday night's clash.

The absence of the 30-year-old is likely to mean that Qarabag FK's Marko Jankovic retains his spot in the starting XI.

Formerly of Preston North End in the Championship, Milutin Osmajic will be looking to add to his 10 senior international goals this month.

A 22-time international, Latvia's Renars Varslavans is sidelined until the New Year due to a serious knee injury.

Last featuring for 11 Vilki in a June friendly against the Faroe Islands, Kristers Penkevics is unavailable for this international window.

Separately, Waze has partnered with Garmin to help improve the safety of cyclists by using real-time location tracking to alert nearby drivers …

Currently, the estimated time of arrival shown in Apple Maps only measures how long it will take you to drive to your destination. The door-to-door time can be significantly longer if it’s going to take time to park and then walk from the parking spot to the final destination.

Code sleuth pdfu has spotted a strong indication that Apple is working on the problem.

Apple may be working on parking-aware ETAs.iOS 27.3 adds a TimeToPark flag. When enabled, CoreRoutine uses background location and motion data to measure how long you spent near your destination before parking, and how long you walked afterwards. It saves both metrics per trip,… pic.twitter.com/gwgwtHvbgy

Drivers may not be expecting to find cyclists on some roads, and where there are high speed differentials or limited forward vision that can take drivers by surprise and leave cyclists at risk. Garmin and Waze have announced a partnership intended to mitigate the problem.

When a cyclist using a Garmin bike computer or smartwatch chooses to opt in to sharing their live track location, it will show up for drivers using Waze.

The feature is available on the Garmin Edge and any Garmin smartwatch when used with a compatible smartphone and the Garmin Connect app.

Valve opens up the doors to the data it's been collecting.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Steam Deck is the most popular handheld gaming PC, and no doubt a lot of you own one already. It's also far from the most powerful handheld on the market, meaning devs have to get optimisation just right to allow the Deck to maintain 60 or even 30 fps. Now, Valve is letting everyone see exactly how well those optimisations are made by listing average Steam Deck frame rate data on game store pages.

Valve explains that this applies to any game that's been tested for Steam Deck compatibility, not just Verified ones. It applies to 'Verified', 'Playable', and 'Unsupported' ones. Apparently that means thousands of games right now.

The data is based on what different users have opted in to provide, which, despite only being for opt-inners, presumably still gives a big sample size. We've known about Valve collecting this kind of data since at least February this year, and we heard about Valve handing it over to game devs just last month.

Now, Valve is letting us all get in on the action. To look at a game's average frame rate data, go to its store page, look on the right-hand side for where it lists its 'Steam Deck Compatibility', and click 'Learn more'.

Once there, you're shown the average fps, and if you click the down arrow, you can view a "framerate histogram... breaking down the percentage of data samples at each framerate." The bigger a particular bar, the more of the game session (on average) is enjoyed at that frame rate.

I checked the Elden Ring data, for instance, and found that the Steam Deck OLED averages 42 fps and the Steam Deck LCD 38 fps. The latter seems mostly split between 60 fps and 30 fps, with most at 30 and little in between, leading me to believe most players lock their frame rate for more stable gameplay.

The biggest benefit, though, will probably just be the ability to quickly view the average frame rate. It will mean less guessing exactly how well a game should perform on your Steam Deck before you decide to purchase. And that can benefit other handheld gamers, too, as if you have a more powerful handheld, you'll know you can achieve at least that frame rate or higher.

Consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, more than expected, complicating Fed's inflation fight

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Costs for goods and services rose more than expected in October, providing fresh warning signs for the Federal Reserve in its fight to lower inflation.

The consumer price index, a broad gauge of costs across the U.S. economy, increased a seasonally adjusted 0.4% for the month, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. Economists surveyed by Dow Jones had been looking for a 0.3% increase.

Stripping out volatile food and energy, so-called core CPI increased 0.3% against the forecast for a 0.2% gain.

The headline CPI had increased 0.6% in September while core was up 0.3%.

The BLS did not estimate a yearly rate. The government shutdown last year prevented the bureau from collecting sufficient data in October 2025, so there is no comparable figure to use. However, the report did include annual figures for some individual items where it was able to collect data.

Stock market futures drifted lower following the release while Treasury yields jumped. Traders fully priced in a quarter percentage point increase when the Fed next meets Dec. 8-9 and put odds of a half-point hike at 13.6%, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool.

The report comes at a critical juncture for the Federal Reserve as it considers its next move regarding interest rates. At its October meeting, central bank officials voted 10-2 to keep their benchmark rate in a range between 3.75%-4% while awaiting incoming data.

"This was the report the market could least afford to see," said Karen Manna, fixed income investment strategist at Federated Hermes. "A hotter inflation print raises fresh questions about whether the Fed has done enough to contain price pressures and reinforces the risk that interest rates need to move higher and stay there for longer."

Though gasoline prices have been falling in recent weeks, the energy index rose 0.7% and the gasoline index was up 1%. The natural gas index, though, was off 0.8%.

Food costs accelerated 0.5%, pushed higher by increases of 1% for snacks, 0.9% for beef and 0.5% for meats, poultry, fish and eggs. Transportation services jumped 0.8%. New vehicle prices increased 0.2% but used cars and trucks fell 1.2%.

Shelter costs were higher by 0.3%, which the BLS said accounted for more than 30% of the headline increase.

Apparel, a tariff-sensitive sector, rose just 0.1%. Medical care commodities prices increased 0.5%. Airline fares accelerated 0.4%.

Markets already had been expecting the Fed to hike its benchmark borrowing rate next month, but the inflation data may have sealed the deal.

Speaking Tuesday morning in London ahead of the CPI release, Governor Christopher Waller said a hot inflation report could push him toward advocating for a half-point increase in December. Waller added that it's important the Fed follow through on expectations for a hike.

Despite the moderating gas prices recently, Fed officials have expressed concern that the impacts from the Iran war and President Donald Trump's tariffs are becoming embedded in the economy. Inflation has been running above the Fed's 2% target for some 5½ years.

In addition to tariffs and the war, inflation has been pressured by hyperscaler spending in the artificial intelligence buildout. Electricity prices rose 0.9%, a potential complication from the AI story.

Consumers, though, continue to spend — retail sales rose 0.8% in September, according to the Commerce Department — and businesses are still raising prices.

A net 37% of businesses responding to the October National Federation of Independent Business survey reported that they plan to raise prices in the next three months, the highest level since July 2022.

Original Harry Potter Star Officially Confirms Franchise Return Ahead of HBO Series

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The release of HBO's Harry Potter is just a few weeks away at this point, and it feels like the entire world is watching and waiting to rejoin the Wizarding World. However, there are a select few who have already been there, and when given the opportunity to head back to Hogwarts, it seems like they just couldn't say no.

Speaking exclusively with Collider's Steve Weintraub while promoting his upcoming movie Ebenezer, Rupert Grint revealed that his return as Ron Weasley in Broadway's Harry Potter and the Cursed Child will probably be the last time he plays the character. The actor is scheduled to begin his limited engagement at New York's Lyric Theatre on February 2, 2027. Grint explained that, after just over 15 years away from the role, it felt like the right time because, not only does he now have a family of his own, but he's also reached roughly the same age as Ron in the stage production. Grint told Collider:

"It's funny, kind of, it's interesting. Kind of going back, I think I spent so much time after finishing Potter moving away from it and doing other things which I really enjoy doing. But I think now it's a very different Ron in a very different context, and weirdly, it's lined up that I'm kind of the exact age of the character in that story. So it just felt like a good time, and I love New York and being on stage. It's been a while since I've done that, and it's a very important thing to me. It was my childhood, and I have a very strong connection to that character and that world. And I think I'm not going to get many other opportunities to play him again. It probably is my last, and I think it'll be fun."

Grint previously made his Broadway debut in It's Only a Play in 2014, but returning to the stage as a character he's known for most of his life presents an entirely different challenge, and he admitted that he couldn't resist the challenge, while confessing the nostalgia had been a delight. "Well, yeah. I mean, it's very nostalgic," said Grint. "Even for me, it's fun to kind of go back and get back into that world. It's been a while." Of course, Weintraub couldn't resist asking the question that just about every Harry Potter fan will be thinking: could Grint persuade Radcliffe and Watson to join him on Broadway for a reunion?

"It would be fun. I don't know. I mean, it's obviously different for everyone. It was an intense kind of time. When we finished, I really enjoyed stepping away and not really even thinking about it. Because it was such an intense... I mean, it was enjoyable, and they looked after us. But it was a big part of us. So yeah, maybe. I don't know. It'd be fun."

Before Grint returns to Hogwarts, audiences will get to see him in Ebenezer, a new adaptation of Charles Dickens' A Christmas Carol starring Johnny Depp (Pirates of the Caribbean) as Ebenezer Scrooge, and directed by Ti West. Grint plays Bob Cratchit, the father of Tiny Tim. The tale has been adapted many times for the screen. It's a thrilling ghost story set in Dickens’ London that follows one man’s supernatural journey to face his past, present, and future — and his fight for a second chance.

In addition to Grint, the film stars Andrea Riseborough (To Leslie) as the Ghost of Christmas Past, Sam Claflin (The Hunger Games) as Fred, Daisy Ridley (Star Wars: The Force Awakens) as Clara, Arthur Conti (Beetlejuice Beetlejuice) as Young Ebenezer, Ellie Bamber (Willow) as Belle, Tramell Tillman (Severance) as the Ghost of Christmas Present, and Ian McKellen (The Lord of the Rings) as Jacob Marley. The screenplay comes from Nathaniel Halpern, with Emma Watts producing. Ebenezer opens in theaters on November 13, 2026.

Archaeologists Drug-Tested a 2,800-Year-Old Nomad—and Found the World’s Earliest Stoner

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Hair from a royal Scythian tomb holds the oldest direct evidence of people getting high on weed.

Some 2,800 years ago, Scythian nomads in what is now southern Siberia were using cannabis to get high, and researchers believe they finally have the evidence to prove it. Exactly how the horse-riding nomads took the drug is still unclear (the best guess is that they unsurprisingly inhaled it), but the find stands as the oldest direct evidence of mind-altering cannabis use anywhere in the world.

“The findings suggest that the plant was used for its psychoactive qualities across Eurasia, where specific wild populations might have had naturally higher active compound levels,” a group of researchers wrote in a study published in the journal Science. “In this study, we present the earliest direct evidence of cannabis use: offerings of plant parts and chemical compounds in ancient human hair recovered from a monumental tomb in Siberia.”

Researchers from the University of Bern and the Max Planck Institute of Geoanthropology studied the tomb of a high-ranking Scythian in southern Siberia. The tomb contained over 1,000 cannabis seeds, but more interesting was the examination of preserved human hair from the central burial chamber showing cannabis consumption.

“The seeds show that cannabis played an important role in the ritual surrounding the burial,” Gino Caspari, an archaeologist and the study’s lead author, said in a statement. “This study is particularly insightful because it combines the analysis of plant remains with molecular traces in the human body.”

Cannabis is the world’s most widely used illicit psychoactive plant, but prehistoric evidence of people consuming it is rare. Plant remains seldom survive for thousands of years, and most of the archaeological record points to cannabis being used for oil and fiber rather than for getting high. To date the Siberian burial, the team radiocarbon-dated six of the surviving seeds, which put the site at about 2,800 years old. They then analyzed the hair for chemical signs of consumption. If the results hold up, they push back the earliest known psychoactive use of cannabis by at least 300 years. The previous record came from a roughly 2,500-year-old site in western China.

The seeds are likely from wild plants, and the hair samples revealed clear signals consistent with cannabinol, produced by the oxidation of tetrahydrocannabinol, the most intoxicating compound in cannabis. “The molecular traces in the hair and the cannabis seeds come from the same grave and suggest that cannabis was used for its intoxicating properties,” Caspari said.

“It’s only so often as an archaeologist that you do get to drug test a person that’s been dead for almost 3,000 years,” Caspari told the Associated Press, adding the 1,000 cannabis seeds are “not there by accident” but instead show that the plant held great significance for the ancient Scythians.

For roughly 2,500 years, the best-known account of Scythian cannabis use came from a Greek writer. Around 440 B.C., Herodotus described Scythians throwing hemp seeds onto red-hot stones inside felt tents and howling with pleasure in the smoke. Archaeologists have since found burned seeds and cannabis-stained braziers that seem to support his story, but direct evidence that ancient people actually took the drug has remained hard to find.

“There is a lot of speculation about the use of psychoactive substances in the past,” Caspari told Scientific American. “It is extremely rare to find real evidence for it. We bring a bit of realism into these debates, and we show that cannabis has a deep cultural history which might factor into ongoing societal discourse on legalization and medical use.”

Not everyone is convinced. Volker Auwärter, head of forensic toxicology at the University Medical Center Freiburg in Germany, told the Associated Press that the hair analysis is “scientifically weak.” Because cannabis was widely grown for oil and fiber, he argued, the presence of the plant doesn’t prove anyone took it as a drug. He also said the hair may have absorbed traces of cannabis from the seeds buried nearby.

Caspari said the team is sequencing some of the plant samples to learn more about the history of the domestication and spread of the cannabis crop. “We show that the practice of cannabis consumption dates back at least 2,800 years,” the authors wrote, “anchoring debates around contemporary usage and informing discussions on cannabis.”

Tim Newcomb is a journalist based in the Pacific Northwest. He covers stadiums, sneakers, gear, infrastructure, and more for a variety of publications, including Popular Mechanics. His favorite interviews have included sit-downs with Roger Federer in Switzerland, Kobe Bryant in Los Angeles, and Tinker Hatfield in Portland.

This flying home security camera has been in the works for a while

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After years in the works, Ring's flying home security camera drone is finally taking flight. The Ring Drone Cam, as it's called, has gone through several iterations since the company first introduced it as the Ring Always Home cam three years ago.

Now that Ring is ready to ship the device, it gave us a sneak preview in advance. Here's my initial impressions of the Ring Drone Cam, as well as how much it'll cost, and when you can get one for your home.

Unlike the Always Home Cam, which had a large protuberance on its underside, the Ring Drone Cam looks somewhat like the DJI Neo; it has a small rectangular body with four encaged rotors. Weighing 265 grams (5.8 ounces), it's lighter than most phones. While the demo unit I saw was white, the actual product will be a light gray. The Drone Cam docks and recharges in a square base station.

The drone has a 2K camera with two LEDs on either side, which it uses to light up your home as it's patrolling at night.

When activated, the Drone Cam lifts up out of its base, rises to about 4-5 feet in the air, and then proceeds at a stately pace as it moves throughout your home. Even though it's small, you'll definitely notice its presence, as its rotors make a very loud, high-pitched whine.

Knowing how my dog reacts to a vacuum cleaner, I can only imagine his response to this thing flying around the house. Don't even ask what my cats might think.

The Drone Cam navigates your home using a combination of an Alexa Home map (similar to what you'll see on one of Amazon's smart displays) and the setup process, whereby you hold the drone and carry it through your house, taking it on a trip of where you'd like to go. Ring says the Drone Cam can cover multiple stories of your home.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Unlike previous, unreleased versions, the Drone Cam relies on the cloud for navigation, using Amazon's AI Pilot to get around and interpret what the drone is seeing. If the drone loses its connection, it's designed to immediately land — which is why it's important that you make sure its flight path doesn't go over any delicate objects.

The Ring Drone Cam can be programmed to make regular patrols of your house, or to respond to a particular event — such as a window or a door sensor tripping, or a water leak detector going off. Using the Amazon Alexa Home map, it can fly to the location of the event, and give you a live view of what's happening.

Remember the Amazon Astro? Ring took its parent company's home robot and redesigned the internals to make it a security robot for small businesses and enterprise customers.

Now called the Ring Rover Cam, it's meant to patrol warehouses and stores, and alert you if it detects an intruder. Like the Astro, it has a telescoping camera, but Ring also added a light on the back, which lets you see where the robot is at a glance, and flashes red and screams out a siren when there's an unwanted guest.

The Ring Rover Cam will cost $999, and will ship on December 16.

I am more than a bit skeptical about having a camera drone flying through my house. For starters, my pets get nervous enough around vacuum cleaners; I can only imagine how they'd react to a high-pitched drone flying about. And, the idea of a drone autonomously roaming around, potentially recording everything, feels a bit dystopian.

And, if you want the Ring Drone Camera to be able to access every corner of your house, you'll also have to make sure you leave every door wide open before you leave. And, the drone moves so slowly that if it's set up on one side of your house, by the time it responds to, say, a window breaking on the other side, the potential intruder could be long gone.

I can see some utility in it — I don't have, nor do I want, security cameras in every corner of my house, so I can see it being handy if a sensor is tripped in my basement. But, I'm still not sold on the concept; maybe trying it out will change my mind.

What do you think? Would you want a drone security camera for the inside of your house? Take our poll, and let me know in the comments.

In Puerto Rico, It's Not Thanksgiving—It's Sanguiving

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Puerto Rican Thanksgiving is an exhilarating holiday remix. Think turkey marinated in garlic, lime, and oregano, then stuffed with fried green plantains and chicharrón mofongo. Gravy is more likely to be made with guava than with giblets, and flan stands in for apple pie. “Pumpkin spice” is nowhere to be found; here, the local winter squash calabaza takes center stage. And in lieu of warm apple cider, sweet coconut water is sipped beachside straight from a coco.

Thanksgiving is just as cherished in Puerto Rico as it is on the mainland. Celebrated locally since the early 20th century, shortly after the United States invaded in 1898, it marks the beginning of the island’s rambunctious holiday season. It is also a favorite of chef and restaurateur Giovanna Huyke, who over four decades has become the feast’s culinary authority in Puerto Rico.

I meet with Huyke, widely considered the Puerto Rican answer to Julia Child, on a hot summer day at SatoFino, a neon-hued, woman-owned restaurant in San Juan. The bartender quickly spies her—Huyke is a regular, and her signature red hair makes her easy to spot—and prepares the chef ’s go-to drink, a stiff Manhattan. We settle into our barstools, readying to plan what will ultimately unfurl into an epic cooking adventure. Huyke has a propensity for them, she tells me. In the preceding days she traveled to Atlanta to cook for Ser Familia—a nonprofit that specializes in supporting Hispanic families—before she rushed back to Caguas, a city 20 miles south of San Juan, where she fed VIPs attending the Miss Universe Puerto Rico pageant. Then she raced across the island to teach a traditional-methods cooking class in San Germán, the second-oldest city in Puerto Rico. Now, she’ll show me how to prepare a Puerto Rican Thanksgiving feast.

A vueltita through the Plaza del Mercado de Río Piedras with a fresh haul of groceries, in preparation for Sanguiving.

“We have a rich and resilient food history—it came from necessity, lack, and a bounty from the land,” Huyke says. It’s a complex cuisine informed by both a deep agricultural heritage and a modern reliance on imported and shelf-stable ingredients, which, despite many obstacles, manages to emerge beautiful and full of flavor—the continuation of a culinary evolution ongoing for centuries. “Our food comes from Spain, Indigenous Taíno people, and [enslaved] West Africans,” Huyke continues. “But it grew from our land and from our history, mixing every influence in our pilónes and our cauldrons.”

Being around Huyke is a joy. A local TV personality, newspaper columnist, and cookbook writer since the late ’80s, Huyke has taught generations of Puerto Rican home cooks how to prepare the island’s most treasured dishes. Among them was my grandmother, Sara, who I call Tata. Like many members of the Puerto Rican diaspora, my family traveled back to the island for the festivities, alternating each year between Thanksgiving and Christmas. We’d enter Hartsfield- Jackson International Airport in Atlanta in heavy winter coats and leave San Juan’s Luis Muños Marín in shorts and tank tops.

Tata’s Thanksgiving table centered on the expected turkey, but her stuffing was exceedingly Puerto Rican. To a base of Pepperidge Farm stuffing mix, she added picadillo: ground beef cooked with sofrito—the fragrant blend of garlic, onions, peppers, and the punchy native herb culantro that is the cornerstone of the Puerto Rican palate—and pimiento-stuffed Manzanilla olives, all seasoned with the golden spice blend sazón. When Tata died I inherited a weathered copy of Huyke’s 2004 recipe booklet Celebraciones. Within it were recipes that clearly informed Tata’s tremendous Thanksgiving menu; it turned out Huyke had been influencing my holiday for even longer than I knew.

Although formally known in Puerto Rico as El Día de Acción de Gracias, in true Boricua fashion, Thanksgiving is often referred to as Sanguiving, a play on words that gestures to the Roman Catholicism that remains the region’s predominant religion. It’s also El Día del Pavo, which directly translates to “Turkey Day,” and Sangiving, spelled like it sounds. We islanders have a fondness for clever, shortened expressions, an example of our natural tendency toward Puertorriqueñizar—in English, “to make Puerto Rican.”

A stall brims with large slabs of local calabaza.

“Our adobo goes on the turkey, and all the sides have something to do with sofrito,” Huyke tells me, walking me through the standard Puerto Rican Thanksgiving table. In Puerto Rico, adobo takes two forms—one wet and one dry. Each is a spectacular blend of Spanish, African, and Indigenous ingredients and techniques—garlic, onion, peppers, and herbs pounded in a wooden mortar and pestle with Taíno roots called a pilón, binding the island’s ancestral and colonial cultures.

Puerto Ricans officially became American citizens (and draft eligible) in 1917, just weeks before the US entered World War I. As many as 20,000 locals served, and Puerto Rican soldiers have played a role in every US military conflict since. In many respects, Thanksgiving traditions rooted en la isla are part of a broader military promise: American soldiers always eat well on Thanksgiving Day. Puerto Rican soldiers brought many wartime foodways home with them, and this combined with government subsidies means that today staples can at times feel military-adjacent—especially given the reliance on shelf-stable foods required by a storm-prone island. Over time, the territory’s once agrarian economy (centered around sugar, coffee, and tobacco exports) was transformed into an industrial manufacturing system. Puerto Ricans still consume many foods like tinned corned beef, Vienna sausages, Spam, club soda crackers, and canned vegetables; roughly 85 percent of all food in Puerto Rico is imported.

When Huyke’s father, Hector, served in the Korean War, he encountered the military’s famously bland instant mashed potatoes. From then on, he flat-out refused the dish except during holidays, when Huyke’s mother, Alice (herself a trained cooking instructor), made an impossible-to-resist version: yellow potatoes slowly simmered in cream, enriched with chicken bouillon, then whipped until fluffy with butter. Today a garlicky variation features on Huyke’s Sanguiving table.

Huyke, for her part, wants to steer Puerto Rican cuisine away from processed ingredients and back toward fresh foods—not only because they’re healthier, but also because they are richer in flavor and better convey the layered story of the island’s culinary heritage. She favors from-scratch dishes that lean on regional bounty, from Indigenous staples like maize, peppers, yuca, and other root vegetables to later introductions like plantains and coconuts.

Ají dulce (sweet peppers) are essential to local cuisines across Latin America and the Caribbean—and Chef Giovanna's Sanguiving table.

The best way to explain, she tells me, is to cook together. A few weeks later Huyke joins me in my own kitchen in North Carolina. I quickly learn that cooking alongside her is a lot like having your own personal instructor. At each step she explains how she’s arrived at a certain preparation and why she uses specific ingredients over others. It’s clearly a familiar role: Education remains a central focus for Huyke, and she counts among her mentees young women like Natalia Vallejo, the island’s first James Beard Award–winning chef. She frequently enlists up-and-coming chefs to cook alongside her and makes herself available for mentoring phone calls. I now feel lucky that I can count myself among them.

Although Huyke auditions new Sanguiving dishes every year, Puerto Rican favorites are always on the menu, many of them influenced by her mother’s recipes. Key among them is yuca stuffing—or “estuffing,” as Puerto Ricans commonly pronounce it. Huyke’s family version, Agüeybaná stuffing, is named for the island’s powerful Taíno chief who led the local people at the time of the Spanish invasion in 1493. Huyke peels and then grates fresh yuca before grinding it to a paste in the food processor. Meanwhile, she instructs me to sauté sofrito in olive oil before adding ground and salt pork, annatto, herbs, and aromatics. It’s all piled into a high-walled dish and baked.

Next we turn our attention to pastelón, a lasagna-like layered casserole of fried sweet plantains and creamy queso fresco. The dish was likely born in New York City, where Puerto Rican and Italian communities converged, before finding its way to the island.

“The Puerto Rican and Italian diasporas were physically close in New York during the ’50s—they were exposed to lasagna and mozzarella,” she explains.

Then come more sides. Gandules, or pigeon peas, are a treasured crop. Though they’re frequently served with rice at Sanguiving and other times of year—arroz con gandules is Puerto Rico’s national dish— Huyke instead opts for an escabèche-style salad that marries gandules with tomatoes, avocado, and herbs in a tangy vinaigrette. Another island staple, canned beets, gets a fresh reinterpretation as Huyke roasts a local-grown variety and tops them with crumbled queso fresco.

Huyke’s Sanguiving turkey rests on a banana leaf bed and is adorned with freshly picked wildflowers from the rainforest.

Many Puerto Rican families choose pernil, or roast pork shoulder, as their Sanguiving centerpiece. It is typically marinated overnight in an adobo of minced garlic, sour orange juice, olive oil, oregano, salt, and pepper. But Huyke sticks to turkey, brining it first in a mixture of salt, brown sugar, dried bay leaves, fresh herbs, and peppercorns before pushing butter under the skin and brushing the bird with a blend of rum, melted butter, and guava jelly. Huyke preserves the drippings for a tangy-sweet guava-infused gravy. She also shows me how to make ajilímojilí, a highly adaptable, herbaceous sauce of garlic, ají dulce peppers (similar to bell but fruitier and more delicate), culantro, cilantro, olive oil, vinegar, and her own signature addition: a copious amount of capers. Guests will drizzle it over nearly every dish on her Sanguiving table, she tells me.

After hours of cooking and using every imaginable tool, from a traditional pilón to a modern Vitamix blender, we sit down to enjoy the fruits of our frying, molding, steaming, sautéing, and roasting. The result is unmistakably an American Thanksgiving spread, refracted kaleidoscopically through a fresh, colorful Puerto Rican lens.

Puerto Ricans have both embraced and resisted US imperialism at various points. There always lingers the question of statehood, which exists alongside the island’s long-standing and active independence movement. As Huyke’s cooking demonstrates, this push-pull relationship only continues to play out in all sorts of dynamic ways.

“I believe every cuisine evolves,” she says. “But traditions should be kept and taught. So we do not lose the roots that make us unique.”

Bitcoin’s ‘best buying opportunity ever’ has arrived: Mark Moss

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin Opportunity Fund co-founder Mark Moss says that with banks and ETFs widening access to Bitcoin he expects a new record high in 2027.

Wall Street’s embrace of Bitcoin has created the “best buying opportunity ever,” according to investor Mark Moss.

Moss co-founded the Bitcoin Opportunity Fund, where he is an advisory partner. In 2025, UK-listed Satsuma Technology appointed him chief Bitcoin strategist.

Moss tells Cointelegraph on the latest episode of Trade Secrets that he first bought Bitcoin in 2015 at about $300, during a recovery from a $1,000 to $200 plunge.

“It was so risky,” Moss recalls of buying back then. In contrast, today, “you have consensus from the US government and Wall Street.”

“But most people still think it’s a scam,” he says.

That skepticism opens buying opportunities as Bitcoin starts to cross what Moss calls the “chasm” between early adopters and mainstream investors. Banks and exchange-traded funds (ETFs) have made Bitcoin easier to buy and financial advisers could help persuade clients who have stayed away previously to consider investing.

Related: Strategy shareholders approve daily dividends for four preferred stocks

Bitcoin briefly climbed above $80,000 after the Democrats took control of the US Congress in the midterm elections, then gave back those gains as tech stocks sold off. By Friday, it fell to $74,762, its lowest level since August and remains in the doldrums at the time of writing.

Despite the selloff, Moss points to the reaction to the $6.3 billion Binance hack as a sign that sellers were running out.

Bitcoin fell to $76,320 after the breach and slid to $74,762 on Friday, erasing its post-election gains. It fell further to about $73,500 late Sunday, its lowest level since August, after Nvidia’s board announced it would investigate reports that CEO Jensen Huang had been targeted by a foreign intelligence effort.

Bitcoin found another bottom after reports of Nvidia CEO’s scandal. Source: CoinMarketCap

Flows into US spot Bitcoin ETFs swung sharply during election week. The funds took in about $75 million on Wednesday, then shed $430 million on Thursday.

History is on the side of hodlers however. Bitcoin gained an average of about 53% in the 12 months after previous US midterms, Moss says, using data going back to 2014. That leaves three completed election cycles in his sample. He says he expects Bitcoin to set a new all-time high in 2027.

Moss thinks the Bitcoin treasury company to own is Strategy (MSTR), but believes that holding Bitcoin yourself is still superior. Strategy began buying Bitcoin in 2020 and turned those purchases into a core part of its business. It has used stock and debt sales to fund its purchases, giving investors a way to bet on Bitcoin through a publicly traded company.

By Nov. 2, Strategy held 849,327 BTC, more than 4% of Bitcoin’s maximum supply.

“I don’t think there’s another publicly traded company that will outperform Strategy over the next ten years,” Moss says.

Strategy has the world’s largest corporate Bitcoin trove. Source: BitcoinTreasuries.Net

Leverage can help a Bitcoin treasury company’s shares outperform the cryptocurrency it holds when its price rises.

But even if Strategy outperforms, Moss wouldn’t trade his Bitcoin holdings for its shares.

Related: 1inch lawyer sees agencies driving US crypto regulation as midterms unfold

Before Moss bought his first Bitcoin, he made a living buying foreclosed properties, fixing them up and selling them. He moved into property development, then built and sold a medical equipment business. He says he put the proceeds into Southern California real estate.

When the market crashed in 2008, he lost it all.

“The reward of being right is not worth the risk of getting it wrong,” he says.

Even with his outlook for Strategy, Moss says he’d keep his Bitcoin and use only a small share of his portfolio to pursue higher returns through treasury stocks.

In Moss’s model, Bitcoin could reach $1 million under certain circumstances, when it competes with stocks, bonds, gold and real estate for a considerable share of investors’ wealth. The model estimates how much those assets are worth, projects how that pool could grow as the global money supply expands and calculates a Bitcoin price based on the share it captures.

Financial advisers could help bring in that money via one client’s portfolio at a time, encouraging them to shift a few percentage points out of stocks or bonds and into Bitcoin. The scenario depends on a critical mass of clients doing that, to the point where all those small allocations add up.

The model also assumes Bitcoin stays secure and decentralized. Asked what could derail the scenario, Moss pointed to a failure that compromised either feature.

Moss says $1 million is possible by 2030... though hodler’s might have to wait until 2032.

Ring’s Indoor Drone Camera Is Back—and Now Powered by AI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

No one's home tonight, but the sensor on your home office window upstairs alerts your phone: “Motion detected.” Maybe it's just your cat, or a flicker of a passing car’s headlights. Instead of shrugging it off, you ask Alexa to send your Ring Drone Cam to check it out.

The tiny, 165-gram drone lifts out of its charging dock, buzzing loudly, and autonomously flies upstairs, giving you a first-person view on your phone’s screen as it scans the room. Since it's dark in the house, the Drone's spotlights kick in, making it look like someone's physically in the home and moving around—a potential deterrent to would-be thieves. All clear? It heads back to its base to recharge.

That's the peace of mind Ring wants to offer with its latest home security solution, which it announced today for $250, alongside the Ring Rover Cam, a $1,000 roving security robot designed to help businesses monitor warehouses and factories. Both fall under the company's new “Ring Robotics” brand. The Rover Cam launches first on December 16, followed by the Drone Cam on January 27, 2027.

The Ring Drone Cam flies out of its charging station to monitor the home.

This isn't the company's first at-home drone camera. CEO and founder Jamie Siminoff says he had the idea for a flying sentry in 2016 before Amazon bought Ring. The company previously announced an indoor security drone in 2020—the Ring Always Home Cam—and nearly launched it last year. But Siminoff says the team kept running into edge cases that forced them to add more sensors. Those additions required more computing resources and made the drone more power- and battery-hungry, and made it harder to fly. The new solution? Move the intelligence off the vehicle and into the cloud.

The Drone Cam has enough smarts to land if it loses internet connectivity, but the rest of its intelligence now sits entirely in the cloud; the drone sends video straight to Ring’s servers, where a custom “AI Pilot” agent tells it where to go. That lets the drone be lightweight and nimble.

It will avoid people and objects, and if you accidentally manage to stick a finger into the propellers, Siminoff says you will feel it, but “you'll be fine.” If your cat manages to swat it out of the air, however, that's a different story. A Ring spokesperson tells WIRED customers shouldn't “fly it” if it's damaged and should contact support for help (no word on repairability). The Drone Cam runs a motor health check before take-off every time and won't fly if it detects a problem.

Users can't manually control the drone, but can set up custom flight routes and patrol schedules. It can automatically respond to alerts from security sensors you may have set up in the home, like motion, door, or window sensors. It will not leave your home, as it needs a Wi-Fi network to operate and uses the Alexa Home Map you may have created with existing Echo devices to help it navigate. It can fly around five to eight minutes per charge. (Also, the final production model will be black, not white like in the pictures.)

“There are times when you do want to just be able to see everywhere around the home, but you don't necessarily want a [fixed] camera," Siminoff says. “That's why we invented Ring Robotics.”

Because it doesn't work outdoors and since it's well below the 250-gram threshold, the Drone Cam doesn't need to be registered with the Federal Aviation Administration. Drones have also struggled in the US after the Federal Communications Commission banned DJI from importing foreign-made drones without certification. But Siminoff says the Drone Cam complies with all US regulations and is assembled in Texas.

But what if a bad actor gets access to the Drone Cam? Siminoff points to the company's TAKE technology, or Throw Away the Key Encryption. It encrypts video in transit and rotates keys every few minutes, but Ring temporarily holds the key to run cloud-based AI features before deleting its copy. That brief server-side access window makes it not as foolproof as true end-to-end encryption. (Every Ring account does require two-factor authentication to log in.)

Hackers have also invaded smart home devices and peeked through robot vacuum cameras, baby monitors, and other gadgets, so it's not hard to imagine a similar situation with the Drone Cam. To counter the threat of surreptitious surveillance, Siminoff says the Drone Cam is loud by design—the extremely loud buzz of its propellers means a bad actor couldn't remotely snoop without tipping off people in the home.

Concerns don't just stem from third parties trying to access your devices, though. Amazon's Ring has been accused of normalizing suburban surveillance, partly stemming from the company's cozy relationship with law enforcement, where it has even handed Ring videos to cops without warrants.

Thorin Klosowski, senior security and privacy activist at the Electronic Frontier Foundation, says a device like the Drone Cam is a personal choice and that people should “think through” how it will make others in the home—or visitors—feel.

“I think the companies who make these sorts of surveillance products are invested in us feeling less safe at home,” Klosowski says. “If there is a way to sell a product that helps alleviate the problem they are creating, I think they will do it.”

The Rover Cam is designed to patrol warehouses and factories.

The telescoping camera pops out and can scope out the situation.

Ring Robotics' second robot is designed for businesses, not consumers. The Ring Rover Cam might look familiar—it shares many of the same hardware guts as Amazon's Astro, the short-lived home robot that didn't do much. Amazon had even pivoted it as a $2,350 security robot for businesses before eventually bricking them 10 months after launch.

Siminoff says the robot was great, but the software needed polishing, and people largely wanted to use Astro for security—that's why the Rover Cam ditches the cute face seen on the previous bot. It has the same AI Pilot as the Drone Cam, which helps it navigate through spaces and avoid obstacles. A telescoping 4K camera pops up to monitor its surroundings, and the Rover beams a light circle to the ceiling so you can always glance up to see where the short bot is on the factory floor.

It responds to sensor alerts, can automatically check an area, features two-way talk, and has a built-in siren. It covers up to 5,000 square feet and returns to its charging dock after its patrol ends (it can do about 30 minutes). It's significantly cheaper than Astro was, but buyers still face the lingering risk that Amazon could pull the plug if Ring Robotics doesn't pan out.

Contemporary Stained-Glass Windows Installed in Notre-Dame, Spanish Man Awarded $1.12 M. for Renaissance Door Discovery, and More: Morning Links for November 10, 2026

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

To receive Morning Links in your inbox every weekday, sign up for our Breakfast with ARTnews newsletter.

WINDOW OF OPPORTUNITY. New stained-glass windows by French artist Claire Tabouret have been quietly installed in the Notre-Dame de Paris cathedral and will be unveiled to the public on December 7, reports Le Figaro. The project, backed by French President Emmanuel Macron, has faced controversy, but has moved forward against all odds, overcoming one legal challenge while still facing another. The six new windows feature a color-rich, figurative telling of the story of the Pentecost and have been placed along the nave’s southern aisle. The windows were made with the Simon-Marq workshop in Reims and for now, they are reportedly hidden behind red curtains. Meanwhile, heritage groups have opposed the project because it requires the removal of existing 19th-century stained-glass windows designed by Eugène-Emmanuel Viollet-le-Duc, who intended them as part of a cohesive homage to Gothic architecture. Related Articles Chicago Crowds Try and Fail to Move Anish Kapoor's Bean, Art Lawyer Michael Ward Stout Has Died, Louvre President Says Mona Lisa's Move Will Be Sad, and More Lucien Smith Cause of Death Announced, French Authorities Investigate Unauthorized Reproductions of Picasso Silver Plates and Gold Medallions, and More: Morning Links for November 6, 2026

BRINGING HOME THE CANADIAN BACON. Things are looking up for Canadian artists who are still grappling with US tariffs. The country has just proposed a federal budget that exempts artists from paying taxes on C$45,000 ($38,000) of their earnings, reports The Art Newspaper. “In Budget 2026, we’re backing the Canadian artists who tell our stories,” wrote Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney in a November 5 social media post. “That means if you’re a Canadian artist, you won’t pay tax on up to C$45,000 of income derived from your creative work,” he added. And that’s not all; the proposed budget also pledges C$783.1 million ($554 million) over five years to the National Arts Centre, Library and Archives Canada, the National Battlefields Commission and national museums. Visitors under 25 will also get free entry to national museums, thanks to C$46.3 million ($32.8 million) in subsidies for the same five years, followed by C$11.5 million ($8.1 million) per year. Not to be forgotten, the Canada Council for the Arts will get C$20 million ($14.1 million) over three years. However, some tell TAN the funding is still short of what is needed. “As a sector, we still have a lot of questions, while many artists need relief now,” said Jenna Faye Powell, interim national executive director of Carfac (Canadian Artists’ Representation/Le Front des Artistes Canadiens).

The 4 a.m., August 28, 1963, handwritten final draft of Rev. Dr. Martin Luther King Jr’s historic “I Have a Dream” speech is being offered for sale at Christie’s New York to benefit charity on January 22, 2027. [ArtDependence]

In an open letter appealing to the Nuremberg City Council, a contemporary arts association has warned of the possible closure of the 1967-founded Kunsthalle Nürnberg amid a strained public budget. [Monopol Magazine]

Artist Olafur Eliasson is partnering with ARoS Aarhus Art Museum in Denmark to create a publicly accessible study space and library containing research materials about Eliasson’s practice, opening alongside a major solo exhibition dedicated to him, plus newly installed artwork and more. [Ocula]

Artist Ryan Gander dishes on the YBA’s “egocentric, attention-seeking, shallow kind of art.” Plus, the act of drawing and painting being “futile and dumb,” despite including several of his own new paintings in his latest show, “Maybe: Ryan Gander” at Towner gallery in Eastbourne. [The Guardian]

WHEN ONE DOOR CLOSES … A decade after, Juan Francisco León Catena, a resident of Úbeda in southern Spain, chanced upon a magnificent Renaissance doorway hidden behind the walls of his property under renovation—he will be awarded a €1 million prize ($1.12 million) for his trouble, reports El País. Following a long legal battle, León Catena is poised to receive the award, granted by the Spanish Historical Heritage Law for chance, significant artistic finds. In this case, that find is a 15th-century portal from a former hospital church designed by Spanish Renaissance architect Andrés de Vandelvira. Discovered in excellent condition, experts deemed it of “great aesthetic value” and a unique Renaissance artwork. However, the local Andalusian Regional Government initially refused to pay up in a case that made its way to the High Court of Justice of Andalusia, which ultimately rejected their arguments. Now, León Catena is in talks with the city of Úbeda on how to best promote the heritage site, and there is the additional question of surrounding, city-owned buildings that also house the relic but are in dire need of restoration and risk collapse.

Amazon’s Ring tries again with a $250 flying home security drone and a $1,000 patrol robot

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 10, 2026 at 5:30 amNovember 10, 2026 at 5:49 am

Amazon’s Ring unveiled a $249.99 flying indoor security drone and a $999.99 mobile patrol robot for businesses, bringing back two ideas that stalled in earlier incarnations.

The Ring Drone Cam and Ring Rover Cam, announced Tuesday, are the first products under the Ring Robotics name, which the company calls “a new category of autonomous, mobile security.” Both devices use a system Ring calls AI Pilot to map a home or business, suggest spots worth checking, and find their own way around obstacles such as furniture and shelving.

The drone stays docked with its camera off until it’s sent out on a schedule, on demand, or automatically when another Ring device is triggered. The Rover patrols stores and offices, using a periscope camera to see over counters and shelving.

The Rover is available for preorder in the U.S. and is scheduled to ship Dec. 16. The Drone Cam is slated for Jan. 27, 2027, but Ring is only taking email sign-ups for now. The fine print notes that the drone hasn’t yet received the required authorization from the Federal Communications Commission.

Scheduled flights and patrols, and reviewing the Rover’s recorded video, require a Ring Protect subscription plan. Ring didn’t say which plans qualify.

Ring says customers wanted to see spots a fixed camera can’t reach, and rooms where they wouldn’t want one mounted.

“The honest answer wasn’t more cameras; it meant reimagining what a security device could be,” said Jamie Siminoff, Ring’s founder and chief inventor, in a statement. “That kind of invention is hard, and it takes time.”

Ring unveiled a flying indoor camera called the Always Home Cam in 2020, also priced at $249.99. The company later opened an invitation list, but the device never went on general sale.

The original drone flew only along paths that owners mapped out with it in advance. Ring says the new one builds a floor plan as the owner carries it through the house, suggests spots to check, and plans its own routes.

The Rover Cam traces back to Astro for Business, a rolling security robot that Amazon launched in November 2023 at a starting price of $2,350. Amazon discontinued it less than a year later, refunding customers and shutting the robots off.

Astro for Business was a spinoff of Amazon’s Astro home robot, an Alexa-powered device on wheels that debuted in 2021 and can patrol the house on its own.

Ring says the new robot was inspired by Astro customers who said roving security was their favorite feature. The Ring team “leveraged the best of Astro” to build it, the company says. Ring is pitching the Rover as “not a home robot that also does security, but a purpose-built security robot.”

The original Astro for the home remains an invite-only product. Amazon has redeployed much of the Astro team to work on a new home robot, CNBC reported Sunday. In March, Amazon acquired Fauna Robotics, a New York humanoid robot startup, saying it would become part of a new Personal Robotics Group. Ring’s announcement doesn’t mention either effort.

The Rover also extends Ring’s push into commercial security, following a $5,000 surveillance trailer for parking lots that it unveiled in January. In security robots, it competes with companies such as Knightscope, which rents its robots through a subscription, and Cobalt AI. At $999.99, the Rover costs less than half what Astro for Business did.

On the security drone front, Indoor Robotics makes a ceiling-docking drone that patrols offices and warehouses, but flying security cameras for the home remain rare.

Siminoff, who launched Ring’s predecessor, Doorbot, in 2012 and sold Ring to Amazon in 2018, left the company in 2023 and returned last year to lead Ring and Amazon’s related home security businesses. Last month, Ring unveiled its first smart lock, a $249 deadbolt with no keyhole and a dial that recharges a dead battery by hand.

Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

These Like-New Kindles Are up to $75 Off Ahead of Black Friday

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Amazon is offering early Black Friday discounts on like-new versions of its 2024 Kindles, with prices starting at $152.99 for the Paperwhite and savings of $27 to $75 across five models that include the color-screen Colorsoft and the pen-equipped Kindle Scribe. All five are a generation behind Amazon's current lineup: The company introduced redesigned Paperwhite and Colorsoft models (plus accessories) in October, with the new Paperwhite now starting at $199.99, and replaced the 2024 Scribe with a third-generation model in December 2025. As for its “like-new” label, it means these are pre-owned devices that Amazon has tested and certified to look and work like new.

The Like-New Kindle Paperwhite (16GB) is down to $152.99 from $179.99 and covers just about everything most people need for everyday reading, including enough storage for thousands of ebooks. Its seven-inch, 300ppi glare-free display is larger than the previous generation’s 6.8-inch screen, with faster page turns, an adjustable warm light, IPX8 water resistance, and up to 12 weeks of battery life. As PCMag notes in its review, it's a great option for readers who don't need color or handwriting features.

The Like-New Kindle Paperwhite Signature Edition (32GB) is down to $190.99 from $224.99, making it just $38 more than the like-new Paperwhite (16GB). For that relatively small difference, you're doubling the storage to 32GB and getting wireless charging, an ad-free lock screen, and a front light that automatically adjusts to your surroundings—on top of the same sharp seven-inch display, IPX8 water resistance, and up to 12 weeks of battery life as the standard Paperwhite, as noted by this PCMag review.

The Like-New Kindle Colorsoft (16GB) is down to $220.99 from $260.99 and offers something the Paperwhite models can’t: a color E-Ink display, which makes it well suited to comics, graphic novels, illustrated books, and travel guides. Its seven-inch screen shows that color content at 150ppi while keeping text at a sharp 300ppi, and you can mark passages in multiple highlight colors to keep your notes organized. That said, colors are noticeably more muted than on a conventional tablet, and battery life tops out at eight weeks, compared with 12 weeks on the Paperwhite.

The Like-New Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition (32GB) is down to $243.99 from $287.99, making it just $23 more than the standard like-new Colorsoft. For that modest increase, you're getting twice the storage at 32GB, which is particularly useful for comics, manga, and graphic novels that take up more space than regular ebooks, along with wireless charging, an automatically adjusting front light, and an ad-free lock screen. However, as PCMag notes in its review, the color display produces more subdued tones than a conventional tablet, so the Paperwhite remains the more economical choice if you primarily read novels.

The Like-New Kindle Scribe (16GB) is down to $284.99 from $359.99, the biggest dollar discount in this roundup. Unlike the other Kindles here, it's built for reading and writing, with a 10.2-inch, 300ppi glare-free display and a Premium Pen with an eraser and shortcut button—making it the most versatile option for readers who want an e-reader that doubles as a digital notebook.

In short, yes, Black Friday still offers discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. If there’s something you want to buy, or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts on what you need, especially tech sales, home improvement supplies, and fitness tech. Of course, if you need to save money, the best way to save is not to buy anything.

Black Friday sales officially begin Friday, November 27, 2026, and run throughout “Cyber Week,” the five-day period that runs from Thanksgiving through Cyber Monday,  November 30, 2026. But Black Friday and Cyber Monday dates have expanded as retailers compete for customers. You can get the same Black Friday sales early, and we expect sales to wind down by December 2, 2026.

Yes, Amazon has Black Friday sales, but prices aren’t always what they seem. Use a price tracker to make sure you’re getting the best deal, or refer to guides like our live blog that use price trackers for you. And if you have an Amazon Prime membership, make the most of it.

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker.

Jordan Fisher Will Step Into Prince’s Shoes (And Strap On A Guitar) For ‘Purple Rain’ On Broadway

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The actor says his love for the rock icon's music is "in the marrow of my bones" as he prepares to play The Kid.

Since the July announcement that a Purple Rain musical would come to Broadway (following an out of town production in Prince’s native Minneapolis), plenty of questions have loomed about the production, but none bigger than the obvious: who would take on the role of The Kid, immortalized by Prince himself in the film of the same name? Explore Jordan Fisher Prince See latest videos, charts and news

Billboard can now reveal the answer: Jordan Fisher, the 32-year old alum of the screen and stage who’s played several leading roles on Broadway in recent years, will take on the Purple One’s role when Purple Rain begins previews at the Majestic Theater on March 12, 2027 (an opening date is set for April 12). Related Dr. Dre Says He's Going 'Off the Grid' for the First Time in His Career: 'I'm Going to Go Away' Tate McRae's 'Housewife' No. 1 on Global Charts for Third Week, Michael Jackson's 'Thriller' on the Prowl Cardi B, Travis Scott, Don Toliver & More to Perform at 'Grand Theft Auto VI' Festival

“I’ve been such a fan of his for such a long time, for a myriad of reasons, and with this show everything that I have ever worked on in my career professionally I am exercising at the highest level: every instrument that I can play, I am playing on stage live; every dance move, every acrobatic thing that I can do, I am doing on stage in three-inch heels,” Fisher tells Billboard. “I get to tap into this material that’s so familiar to people, but presented in a way that’s fresh for a new generation who maybe didn’t grow up with Purple Rain or with a real understanding of who Prince is and why he is so loved and respected. He is your favorite artist’s favorite artist. He is your favorite musician’s favorite musician, and that kind of mantle is something that I I take very, very seriously.”

Fisher has taken on increasingly demanding leading roles on Broadway in recent years, including John Laurens/Philip Hamilton in Hamilton, the title role in Dear Evan Hansen, Orpheus in Hadestown, and Antony in Sweeney Todd. But The Kid is his most high-profile yet, a role he’ll originate in a sure to be much-scrutinized new musical. On the day he speaks to Billboard, Fisher — wearing a fresh purple and green manicure, not an homage to Prince but the result of a fun trip to the salon with his nephew — is on a break from rehearsal during the show’s second week of development (rehearsals for the Broadway production officially begin in February). He opened up about why he took on such a monumental role, the time he just missed meeting Prince, and getting encouragement from Lin-Manuel Mirandaas he prepares to lead the band on the Broadway stage.  How did you get introduced to Prince’s music, and why do you think it grabbed you as a young person? I mean, there’s so many avenues that we can go down here. I’m five eight and light skinned-ed; I’m an artist and I’m a multi-instrumentalist and I craft and build and create and produce and write and have a very similar kind of pursuit of new and doing things differently. Of course, by no stretch of the imagination would I ever compare myself or my artistry to Prince Rogers Nelson, but [those similarities have] kind of been impressed upon me for a very long time.  You don’t work in the music industry without hearing people talk about this man and the way that he broke rules left and right and stood up for what he believed in, how he loved his city and and yearned for awareness of the magic that was coming out of [Minneapolis].  The genesis of [my own love of] Prince — my grandfather, who’s no longer here, really loved him. He was the first person to really open my eyes and ears to him and his music. I didn’t really get it when I was younger, and when I when I started digging into it and making [my own] music, people started being like, “Oh my God, you must be a huge Prince fan, huh?” Initially, I was like, “Yeah, yeah, because I’m short and I play a bunch of instruments and we look alike?” But no, it’s all these other things I was missing, and I’ve carried that with me in a massive way.   The very first song of his that I ever heard was “I Wanna Be Your Lover,” and I mean, it’s in the marrow of my bones.  So many people in the entertainment world have a Prince story. In your years working in music in Los Angeles did you ever find yourself in a room with him?  I have a story for you, and it’s just gut-wrenching. I have a friend who had a residency at the W Hotel right across from the Pantages for a long time, and I was there every Sunday pretty much, singing back-up, singing some songs myself, in the midst of auditioning for different things and whatnot. One Sunday I was not there — I was at like, a friend’s wedding or something. [Prince] showed up around 1 A.M. He stood up in the middle of a song — because he’s Prince and he could — and he walked onstage and pointed at the guitar player’s guitar, [and he] realized it was Prince and goes, “Here you go,” and then [Prince] played. It was a moment nobody will forget, and I was not there for it!  But a few years later, I did get a chance to play with Sheila E for a show in LA, and that was the closest that I had been to his artistry and to his excellence, and it oozed off of her, right? That’s the closest I was able to get to him until this [show]. And now I’m like, talking to Bobby Z on a regular basis, and Lisa and Wendy and all of these people that like are in our space here?! Nothing like it.  You were called to audition for this production — did you have any skepticism or did you immediately want to do it?  There is a right way and a wrong way to tell this kind of story, and I think that there’s plenty of right ways to make it happen, but I do think that one of the biggest North Stars is the music and how that happens and how that’s presented. When I got the call, I was like, “Well, I gotta  give this thing a shot.”  When I saw [director Saheem Ali’s] name on the breakdown, I was like, “Oh, they got it right!” Like they got it right because he knows how to keep music at the core and tell a story that is human and tangible and for everyone. Like if you go see Buena Vista Social Club [the Broadway musical, which Ali directed], you don’t need to speak Spanish to understand exactly what’s happening, and that is magic. I have so much love for that man, and  I don’t have enough great things to say about him. He is the perfect director for this.

And then Ebony [Williams], our choreographer, is a commercial dancer and choreographer with commercial background — she approaches choreography from a commercial perspective that’s still driven by storytelling.  And then Jason Michael Webb is our music supervisor, and he and I have worked together a handful of times in the past, and I know his process but more than anything, I know that man is the biggest Prince fan I’ve met in my life.  He’s a walking encyclopedia of Prince. Those pieces right there are the key to presenting this show in the best way possible.  You’ve been lucky enough to do a few big original musicals, including Hamilton, and I know you and Lin-Manuel Miranda are friendly — I’d imagine he’s excited about the idea of a Prince musical?  Yeah he is. He’s got Warriors happening right now too [on Broadway in 2027], he’s in the thick of all of that. But I mean, he’s my homie. I’m hard pressed to think of any human being that could ever give somebody advice on playing Prince on Broadway, but we hung out about a month or so ago. We were talking about it, and he was just like, “Man, I know you’re gonna kill it.” That’s really all that you can say to somebody that’s about to take this on.  Do you feel like Prince’s music is well-suited to Broadway? Prince was probably one of the best musical theater writers that never wrote a musical. His songs have a clear story; there’s a clear “want,” nothing is ever accidental, everything is curated from the lyrics to the notes to the solos to the chord structure to the kind of microphones he used, he had it all down to a science. I wish he could experience the way that his music really tells this beautiful story [in the show]. We’re exploring some of that stuff today [in rehearsal] with “Computer Blue” and “When Doves Cry.” It’s hard to believe that this music works in the musical theater canon the way that it does so successfully. Like it’s not just a jukebox musical; the songs actually carry the show.  How are you approaching the vocals — how much of the “voice” is Jordan vs. Prince? This is a constant conversation. I’m pretty good at impressions, I’m not gonna lie. I’m a nerd and an actor, it’s just part of who I am, it’s like one of my love languages — if I know you and I love you, I’m gonna build an impersonation of you. But the presentation of this — I’m not Prince, I’m The Kid. This is a fictional story, it’s not like a biography of the man.  Therefore, his voice is his voice. My voice needs to encapsulate the essence of who he is but still be a fictional character; otherwise I’m just doing an impression on stage and that isn’t necessarily what we’re going for. The voice he uses in the film doesn’t necessarily work on the stage. So it’s the math of all that, and how do I make it sound like not a musical theater thing but let it have enough presence so that I can reach those people in the back of the mezzanine?  To be continued!
