How to Use an Axe Safely

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 10, 2026 1:29 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

Ever since someone put a club and stone together to make the first axe, it has remained a handy tool that has made life easier. Sure, you could fire up a chainsaw to do many things that an axe can do — like chopping wood for fuel, creating wood sculptures, prepping building materials, and clearing land, but an axe doesn’t require any fuel, is way easier to transport, and quieter.

While it may be one of the original tools that humans used, it’s still a tool that comes with risks and it’s important to know how to use an axe safely. It’s also crucial to know the differences among the various axes so you know which one will best suit your needs and how to use it safely and with the right technique.

It’s helpful to know the names for the main parts of an axe so that it’s easier to compare various models, and decide which type is right for your needs. Here are some key axe parts:

It’s not hard to imagine how things can go really wrong when using an axe. In addition to practicing with the axe so you feel confident using it, there are guidelines you should follow to stay safe.

While it may seem like power is the most important part of successful axe-swinging, control and technique have greater influence on success. Even though the weight of the axe helps increase the force, trying to swing an axe that’s too heavy for your abilities is never a good idea. You’ll have better success if you reach for an axe that’s a comfortable weight.

No matter the task, the technique for using an axe is similar.

Most felling strokes are diagonal, but some are horizontal to create a notch in the tree so it will fall. Always position your body (or halt your swing) so that the follow-through does not hit your body. And never stand in the path of where the tree may fall.

The Fiskars X27 is 36 inches long and weighs 6.3 pounds; requires strength to wield but cuts decisively. Read the review.

Limbing can be more dangerous than felling because you are dealing with smaller pieces and a great deal of sharp force near your body. Often, limbing happens while you are standing on a fallen tree. But if you aren’t, make sure there is ample distance between you and the arc of the axe swing. It’s usually easiest to start at the bottom of the tree and work your way to the top branches, swinging in the direction of the top of the tree as you go.

After a downed tree is limbed, it’s ready to be bucked into transportable lengths. Make sure the log won’t roll before you begin. Then, stand on the log and strike the axe below and between your feet.

Cutting logs along the grain is called splitting. A split log dries out faster, and will be ready quicker for a fireplace or fire pit. After placing a log vertically on a chopping block, aim at the face and swing the axe to make contact.

A competitive activity that’s become popular in recent years, axe throwing is usually done in a controlled space. While standing with your nondominant leg forward and at least 12 feet away from the target, pull the axe back over your head. With arms slightly bent, release the axe forward. Just like in golf, follow-through is essential.

Your axe will stay in great shape for a long time with proper care and maintenance. It only takes a few minutes after each use to keep it in fine shape. Plus, it will benefit from oiling and sharpening once a year.

Safely using an axe depends on careful planning about where and when you use the tool. It’s essential to select the right axe based on the task. And it takes practice to know how to hold an axe and how to swing an axe with ease. Never attempt to swing an axe if you feel nervous, if it’s dark out, or if people or pets are within your swing zone.

After months of scouring the market and putting products through their paces, we’ve named the best of the best in new tools. There’s something for everyone, from veteran pros to average Joes.

Chrome zero-day whack-a-mole continues as 2026 tally hits 8

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Another Chrome memory bug gets squashed, but not before attackers got their hands on it

Google has patched its eighth actively exploited Chrome zero-day of 2026, matching its total for the whole of last year with nearly two months still left on the calendar.

The latest flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-125350, is a high-severity use-after-free vulnerability in Chrome's XML component. Google confirmed in a security advisory published Monday that attackers are already exploiting the bug, although it hasn't said who's behind the attacks or who might be getting targeted.

"Google is aware that an exploit for CVE-2026-125350 exists in the wild," the company said, offering little else about the attacks.

The vulnerability was reported anonymously on November 6 and involves a memory management error that occurs when software continues using a pointer to memory that has already been freed. Such bugs can potentially allow attackers to corrupt memory and execute malicious code, although Google hasn't disclosed how this particular flaw is being exploited.

Google is keeping further technical details under wraps until enough users have installed the fix, a familiar approach intended to stop other attackers from getting their hands on a working exploit.

The patch arrives in Chrome 157.0.8103.50/.51 for Windows and macOS, and 157.0.8103.50 for Linux. The update also addresses eight other security vulnerabilities, including high-severity flaws affecting WebGL, FileSystem, GPU, Compositing, and the V8 JavaScript engine.

The release fixes another use-after-free bug in Chrome's XML component, CVE-2026-125346, which was reported by Arni Hardarson of Neonix Security in October, though Google hasn't indicated that this second vulnerability is being exploited.

The latest discovery adds to what's becoming a rather long list of Chrome zero-days this year. By June, Google had already patched five actively exploited Chrome zero-days, including flaws in its V8 JavaScript engine, Skia graphics library, and WebGPU implementation. The list has only grown since then, with two more V8 bugs fixed in September and the latest XML vulnerability bringing the total to eight.

That puts 2026 level with 2025, when Google also fixed eight Chrome zero-days exploited in the wild. Whether the browser maker can make it through the remaining weeks of the year without adding another to the pile is anyone's guess.

Google says the latest update will roll out over the coming days and weeks, but users can check manually by heading to Chrome's Settings menu and selecting About Chrome. With attackers already making use of the vulnerability, it's probably worth doing that sooner rather than waiting for Chrome to get around to it. ®

Skydance to Embrace Bundling Across Business Functions

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

$73 million – Paramount Skydance’s third-quarter net earnings before it acquired Warner Bros. Discovery in October.

$7 billion – Paramount Skydance’s revenue, a 5% year-over-year growth, before its acquisition was completed.

$6 billion – How much Skydance expects to achieve in run-rate synergies over the next three years.

20% – Rise in sports advertising commitments year-over-year for current and upcoming sports seasons leading into 2027.

Over the next few months and possibly years, bundling will take place at Skydance. After completing its acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery in October, the combined conglomerate is now looking to slim down and cut its roughly $80 billion debt.

In corporate speak, Skydance is looking to consolidate a lot of back-end and front-end operations. In regular parlance, this is just bundling, and the media company is looking to bundle much of what it has now to achieve these savings. It has started more than 20 of what it calls “workstreams” to identify areas of possible cuts.

The Skydance third-quarter earnings letter to shareholders said the company aims to reduce costs at least $2.2 billion by “bundling” redundant back-office and corporate functions. It aims to “bundle” its media planning and buying across business units to eliminate audience overlap, coordinate campaigns across those units and maximize media efficiency at scale.

Other areas will also see bundling, including technology. Skydance will unify its enterprise tech stack, streaming ecosystem and broadcast architecture, merging parallel technology functions. It will also bundle procurement and vendor management operations, saying its combined purchasing levels will provide economies of scale by consolidating purchasing decisions into one centralized group.

“This is a growth story; we are shifting from legacy media to building a next-generation media and entertainment company powered by creativity and technology,” said Ynon Kreiz, Skydance’s co-chief executive officer.

At $14,000, Is This 1993 Toyota MR2 A Red-Hot Deal?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

According to its seller, today's Nice Price or No Dice MR2 is a highly optioned model and was once sold on Bring a Trailer. It's been demoted to a Facebook Marketplace ad, and it's up to us to decide whether its price matches that lower-tier venue.

Rumored federal rollover safety standards effectively killed off convertible production in the U.S. from 1976 to the early '80s. The convertibles that boldly remained on the market during this time were all older cars like the MGB and Mercedes-Benz SL.

It was that latter convertible, the two-seater Benz, that served as the role model for the 1977 Cadillac Seville Milan we looked at on Monday. A custom-bodied two-seater convertible, the Milan attempted to fill that obvious gap in North America's car lineup. Unfortunately, the car's odd proportions gave the impression that upon arrival, its occupants would be revealed to be a congress of clowns rather than a chic couple with wind-tousled hair. That factor, plus questions about the car's condition, made us all question the seller's set $8,000 asking price. In short order, that dropped in an 82% 'No Dice' loss.

When it comes to styling and proportions, it's safe to say that Toyota hit the mark with the second-generation MR2. Larger, more cohesive, and roomier, the SW20 edition traded the first MR2's odd mini-Ferrari Mondial look for one more reverent to the better-looking Ferrari 328 GTB. It still checked all the boxes that made the first generation popular: a mid-engine layout, pop-up headlamps, and an optional T-top roof. Where the second-generation car strayed from the first was in size (it's substantially larger in almost all dimensions), and in the breathing of its top-models as the earlier cars' supercharger was traded for a turbo. One other distinction of the SW20 (perhaps unfairly) is that its handling at the limit can bite unwary drivers on the butt.

This 1993 MR2 comes in the officially named Super Red, making it a perfect accompaniment to the Man of Steel's cape and booties. This is a non-turbo car, so horsepower is limited to 135 ponies from the 5S-FE DOHC four. Fortunately, that engine is mated to a five-speed manual to make the most of that output. Even better, the seller says a new clutch was installed 46k miles ago, and the timing belt and water pump were refreshed just 26,000 miles back. New motor mounts, pads and rotors, and T-top seals are additional milestones in the car's service history.

This MR2 looks the part of a well-cared-for car, too. According to the seller, it was bought in Florida and spent much of its life in the South before being brought to New Jersey in 2018. When the present owner made that move, the car had 118,000 miles on the clock. It now has 136,788 miles, which averages about 2,400 miles a year under the current owner's care.

Overall, the car looks to be in great condition. The paint still pops, set off against factory alloys that show no evidence of injury. The engine looks cramped under its cover, and the sweeping buttresses make maintenance even more of a back-breaker, but at least it all looks intact.

The cabin doesn't appear cramped at all and looks quite handsome in its black-and-biscuit color scheme, with swanky leather upholstery on the seating surfaces. It all appears stock in here, save for a TRD-branded leather shift knob, although that does keep it in the family.

On the downside, the seller warns that the A/C compressor is grinding and hence they've removed the belt to keep it from turning and potentially doing more damage. The ad also makes no mention of the tires' age or condition. With so few miles driven each year, it's easy to let tires hang on longer than they should. The MR2 features a staggered setup, with larger, wider wheels in the back, meaning that rotating front to rear is out of the question, making the meats even more out of sight, out of mind.

On the plus side, the ad notes that all of the car's glass has been VIN-etched as an anti-theft measure. In addition, the car comes with a clean title, not one but two options for T-top storage, and three keys. Oddly, though the seller says this was a heavily optioned car, it does not seem to have a remote-entry key fob. Weird. There are also two manila folders full of maintenance records going back to the previous owner. Those will accompany the car in the sale.

On the face of it, this appears to be a solid example of a stock, mostly complete second-gen non-turbo MR2 with all the right specs and the promise of a well-managed service history. What's not to like, right? Well, there's that sweaty-Betty non-working A/C. That's going to cost a pretty penny to fix. We should also probably consider a new set of tires unless, upon inspection, they still seem to be in the game. Other than that, there don't seem to be any major red flags on this car. Plus, who doesn't dig an old MR2?

That only leaves us with the car's $14,000 asking price to either rejoice or bemoan. Let's talk about that. What's your opinion on this MR2 at that $14,000 asking? Does that sound fair given the car's presentation and specs? Or, at that price, is this Mister a miss?

Morning Minute: Vitalik Recasts ETH as a Store of Value

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Morning Minute: Vitalik Recasts ETH as a Store of Value

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🏛️ Vitalik Recasts ETH as a Store of Value

Vitalik Buterin spent Sunday on X telling Ethereum holders to stop valuing ETH as a fee-burning token and start valuing it as a store of value. “We should not feel sad if fee burn is less than 10% of breakeven indefinitely,” he wrote. “The value of ETH comes from elsewhere.”

That elsewhere is stability, which he ties to decentralization, censorship resistance and security. It replaces the ultrasound money pitch, the idea that network usage burns supply faster than staking creates it. The burn has not kept up in years. Through the first nine months of 2026 it offset about 2% of issuance while supply grew 0.64%, mostly because Ethereum’s own rollup roadmap moved activity and fees off the mainnet.

Buterin’s argument is that this is fine, and that chasing the burn would be worse. Maximizing fee revenue means wanting congestion and expensive blockspace. He warned it would turn Ethereum into “a world-scale major corporation” at the cost of what makes it worth using. The burn, he added, was “never a true floor” and came mostly from internal crypto churn like lending and “silly NFT purchases.”

Supporters are already building out the new frame. Ethlabs co-founder Binji Pande calls ETH a future-proof store of value and argues it beats Bitcoin on quantum and AI risk, citing eleven years of trading history and an Ethereum Foundation that holds under 0.2% of supply. Julian Ma of Ethlabs makes the related case that Ethereum’s security culture positions it as a programmable store of value as AI pressures cryptography.

Here’s the problem with the store-of-value pitch, though. Store of value is Bitcoin’s category, and Bitcoin holds it with a hard cap, more Lindy, deeper ETF plumbing and a corporate treasury bid ETH is only starting to build. And now Zcash is creeping in as the privacy store of value play. ETH hasn’t historically been considered a store of value play. It remains to be seen if Vitalik can successfully cast this pitch…

Sweden manager Graham Potter will work closely with Tottenham when it comes to managing Dejan Kulusevski’s return from injury - pledging to have “a close dialogue” with Spurs.

Potter has managed Sweden’s national side since October 2025, five months after Kulusevski’s devastating kneecap injury.

Kulusevski played for the first time in 526 days with an appearance off the bench against Coventry City last month, so Potter has finally been able to call-up the winger for the first time.

The former Juventus man has played just 42 minutes across four games as Spurs carefully manage his return.

Ben White says England fans may not have booed him 'if they knew the full story' over squad exile

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

White walked out of the World Cup camp in Qatar and subsequently made himself unavailable for the rest of Sir Gareth Southgate’s time in charge

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Ben White believes England supporters would not boo him if they knew the complete story behind his departure from the 2022 World Cup squad.

However, the Arsenal defender maintains that he has no intention to "throw anyone under the bus" by speaking out about the incident.

White left the national camp in Qatar citing "personal reasons", following a reported fallout with assistant manager Steve Holland, and subsequently ruled himself out of selection for the remainder of Sir Gareth Southgate’s time in charge.

Though he returned under Thomas Tuchel in March, White was booed by supporters before injury ruled him out of this summer’s World Cup.

His comeback was eventful during a friendly against Uruguay, where he scored and later gave away a penalty.

“I think before the game, I was thinking, just don’t…not stand out, but just nice and easy, you know,” he added.

“Then I came on and obviously get booed. Then I score. Then I give away a pen. So it was obviously full of emotions that night, and it was a good night.

“Everyone can do whatever they want. You know, it’s fine with me. Whatever they want to do, and I think just think about maybe not knowing the full story.

The 29-year-old says he stands by his decision of four years ago, but is looking forward to his “story continuing”.

“It’s something that I’ve not spoken about, and I won’t go into detail about it,” White told talkSPORT. “Yes, it’s a decision that I don’t want to be talking about anyone negatively or throwing anyone under the bus.

“It was obviously not an ideal situation, and (a decision) that I probably still would make.

“There’s loads of things that happened and communication wasn’t great.

“It’s something that I don’t want to throw anyone under the bus. I think there’s a lot of people who tried to do that to me. So it’s something that I wouldn’t want to do to someone else.”

He added on BBC Radio 5 Live: “I don’t think I would (change decision).

“Obviously, I was a bit younger. I’m a bit older now. I think, as a person, I’m quite real, and I think I made the decision that I would probably continue to make.

“It’s one that I’m not happy about, but obviously it happened, it’s time to move on.

“It’s such a privilege to ever get close to playing for your country, something I probably didn’t even dream of because I didn’t think it would be anywhere near my reality.

“There’s been a few bumps, I’m obviously here now, and looking forward to this story continuing.”

The basis of White’s alleged falling out with Holland is thought to be over an interview where the Arsenal defender admitted to not watching football on television.

He said: “To be fair, I don’t. I don’t know if I actually said I don’t like it because people say I don’t like football, which is obviously not true.

“It’s because I don’t watch it, which I think is quite normal for someone who does a job and then doesn’t go home and then continue watching that job.

“But I think it’s my way of release from the game because it’s so intense, obviously.”

Any Bitcoin recovery faces a cash problem as Binance’s stablecoins fall to $32 billion

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In one November 10 sample around 10:42 UTC, Binance had a greater value of Bitcoin buy orders than OKX within 0.05% below each venue’s midpoint. The $32 billion reserve figure covers ERC-20 stablecoins only.

Binance's tracked ERC-20 stablecoin reserves stand near $32 billion after a substantial contraction, giving traders a reason to test the exchange's remaining buying capacity against actual Bitcoin orders. A November 10 comparison with OKX showed Binance had a higher total value of nearby Bitcoin buy orders in one narrow slice of the market, even as its tracked stablecoin balance remained below October levels.

Community analyst Novaque Research via CryptoQuant put the balance at roughly $32 billion on Nov. 10, down from about $41 billion to $42 billion in late October. Its reserve comparison also put OKX near $9 billion, with balances trending lower there. That offers little support for a simple reading that Binance's lost tracked reserves have reappeared at that rival.

Bitcoin was trading around $74,000 on Nov. 10. Coinbase's BTC-USD ticker recorded a last trade of $74,056 at 10:41:58 UTC. The reserve observations arrive after the Oct. 29 hack at Binance and subsequent reopening of withdrawals, which CryptoSlate covered last week.

In a separate Nov. 9 post, community analyst oinonen_t via CryptoQuant reported a 25.6% decline in Binance's ERC-20 stablecoin reserves from an early-October starting point to 32 billion units. The same analysis reported a smaller 7.6% drop in Bitcoin reserves to approximately 673,000 BTC.

Those figures suggest a larger proportional retreat in the tracked stablecoin balance. However, they use an early-October comparison, while Novaque's latest post refers to late October. The exact synchronized dates and wallet coverage are unspecified, so combining them would overstate what is known about the effect of the hack.

There is also a network boundary. ERC-20 balances exclude stablecoins held on other networks. Novaque cautions that wallet restructuring and cross-chain transfers may explain part of the contraction. CryptoSlate's earlier reporting on Binance's Tether routing likewise showed why a decline in identified Ethereum balances does not establish an equivalent withdrawal of customer dollars.

CryptoQuant defines exchange reserves as coins held in exchange addresses and describes stablecoin reserves as potential buying power. Potential matters here: an inventory figure does not say how much is being offered to buy Bitcoin at a particular price.

Around 10:42 UTC on Nov. 10, a snapshot of Binance's public BTC-USDT book showed approximately 1,980,000 USDT of displayed buy orders within 5 basis points, or 0.05%, below its midpoint. The near-contemporaneous OKX book showed about 980,000 USDT within the same width below its own midpoint.

The midpoint is halfway between the highest buy price and lowest sell price. Summing each eligible buy order's price multiplied by its Bitcoin quantity gives the quoted USDT value. Both returned books extended beyond the measured band, allowing a complete comparison at that width. The amounts are in USDT, distinct from Coinbase's US-dollar trade.

Binance therefore displayed more buying interest at those nearby prices in this sample. But orders can change before execution, and one pair of observations cannot establish enduring depth, deterioration from an earlier period or a return to pre-hack conditions. It measures what was displayed then, rather than how much Bitcoin buyers actually purchased.

ERC-20 inventory excludes other networks. No pre-hack depth baseline or execution series is available; these views cannot establish restored liquidity.

Customer backing answers another question. Binance says its proof of reserves concerns assets held in custody for users. That measure does not identify which balances customers have committed to Bitcoin buy orders. Customer inventory, Binance's corporate resources and posted buying orders cannot be treated as one interchangeable pool.

The order-book snapshot preceded the scheduled US inflation release. The Bureau of Labor Statistics calendar listed October CPI for Nov. 10 at 8:30 a.m. Eastern, or 13:30 UTC.

For holders assessing a rebound, stronger executed spot buying alongside buy orders that are replaced as sellers execute would challenge the concern that the smaller tracked stablecoin balance means impaired trading liquidity. Continued reserve contraction accompanied by persistently thinner depth at comparable widths would strengthen that concern.

London’s Photographers’ Gallery to launch exhibition programme for emerging voices

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Photographers’ Gallery has become increasingly reliant on ticket sales as a source of income, its director says

© Dennis Gilbert. Courtesy of the Photographers’ Gallery

The Photographers’ Gallery in London is launching a 10-year programme to platform emerging talent through a series of biennial exhibitions. The initiative has been made possible by what the not-for-profit gallery describes as “a very generous grant” from the UK-based John Kobal Foundation.

Shoair Mavlian, the director of the Photographers’ Gallery, declined to specify how much the grant is worth, but she tells The Art Newspaper that it will cover all the costs of staging the shows in its second-floor space, newly named the John Kobal Foundation Gallery.

The initiative will see one artist selected every two years for an exhibition. The first solo exhibition will be announce later this year and will run from 23 June to 26 September 2027.

“The whole idea of the programme is based around giving an opportunity to someone who hasn’t had an institutional solo show before,” she says. “The Photographers’ Gallery is in a similar situation to all other museums in the UK in that we are pressured into having to always do blockbuster shows [to generate revenue]. And what this programme allows us to do is to take risks.”

The gallery received a renewed three-year grant from Arts Council England in 2023, as one of the funding body’s national portfolio organisations (NPO). This grant represents around 20% of the gallery’s revenue, says Mavlian, however, it has remained the same throughout years of rising operational costs and high inflation, and so like other UK arts organisations, the gallery has become more reliant on other sources of income. Its accounts for the year ending 31 March 2025 report £620,573 in admissions income, which is more than two thirds the value of its NPO grant within a single year.

“We are under this constant pressure to sell tickets and bring people through the door—which, of course, is important. But what that means is we then don’t always see the full breadth of photography that is being made today. We are often looking at things that are historic rather than looking at the contemporary.”

The John Kobal Foundation was established in 1990 a year before the death of the eponymous film historian and photography collector “to advance the public appreciation and awareness of photography”.

“We are delighted to support this important new initiative," says Michael Mack, the co-chair of the John Kobal Foundation, “which honours John’s vision of nurturing creativity and innovation in photography.”

Going deep on local AI performance benchmarking with Apple’s Mac Studio with M5 Ultra taking on Nvidia DGX Spark

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Is a Mac Studio a better investment than a DGX Spark?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Apple’s updated Mac Studio with the M5 Ultra SoC has already proven itself as one of the most powerful systems around, thanks to its 36-core CPU and 80-core GPU with Neural Accelerators for the matrix math operations that drive AI compute. Beyond that raw power, options for up to 512GB of unified memory have many local AI enthusiasts lining up for the 13 to 15 weeks it’ll take for Apple to ship one right now.

But is our tested Mac Studio config worth a whopping $12,300 or more for those folks? We already tested the maxed-out M5 Ultra chip with the popular Qwen 3.8 27B in our initial review, but that compact dense model doesn’t come anywhere close to exercising 256GB of memory. We wanted to fill that pool to the brim with cutting-edge large models like DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 and GLM 5.3 Flash.

We also have some new benchmarks at our disposal to see how the Mac Studio stacks up in agentic tasks on the GPU side in the wake of our review of the Microsoft Surface Laptop Ultra with Nvidia’s RTX Spark chip. So there's much to dig into.

We started our more detailed local AI testing with Artificial Analysis' AgentPerf-Local benchmark. This test plays back recorded inference traces with certain models across a range of agentic AI workloads to gauge performance. Artificial Analysis provides a full breakdown of this workload, but the bulk of its work takes place in prompt processing rather than token generation. As a result, it should favor systems with lots of raw compute, which both GB10 and the M5 Ultra have at their disposal.

The M5 Ultra's 80-core GPU dwarfs GB10's 48 Blackwell SMs at a high level. Even without detailed info on clocks, it would be extremely difficult for GB10 to outgun the M5 Ultra in this test. And the Mac's 1.2 TB/s of memory bandwidth gives it just over four times as much decode bandwidth as GB10's 273 GB/s. But as our past tests have shown, raw specs aren't as important as delivered performance with AI. Delivered performance can vary greatly from paper specs.

We continued to use the dense Qwen 3.8 27B for this test. We used llama.cpp as our inference backend on both the Mac Studio and a single Dell Pro Precision GB10 box, which is nearly identical to the DGX Spark. We further enabled Qwen 3.8 27B’s native multi-token prediction (MTP) support for a decode speed boost on both platforms.

Versus a single DGX Spark-like system, the M5 Ultra Mac Studio finishes this agentic test in just under half the time, an extremely impressive result. One might think this is a silly comparison given the imbalance in compute resources and memory bandwidth between these two systems, but the cost of a single 4TB Dell Pro Precision GB10, like the one we tested, is now a whopping $10K, so comparing it to the $12K Mac Studio head-to-head isn’t actually that far-fetched.

Because AgentPerf-Local performs a fixed amount of work on every system, we can also directly compare power efficiency between platforms by recording the wall power consumed by a single Spark and the Mac Studio and converting it to task energy in kilojoules.

At least with llama.cpp, the Mac Studio not only finishes the AgentPerf-Local test much faster than the DGX Spark, but it also consumes less task energy overall — about 16% less. A different inference engine might produce a more favorable result for the Spark-like system, but apples-to-apples, this test looks fantastic for the Mac Studio.

We can multiply both task time and energy consumed (in kilojoules) together to arrive at the energy-delay product (EDP) for each system. A lower EDP wraps up a fast time to completion and a low task energy into a single comparable figure for efficiency.

The Mac Studio’s EDP is under half that of the DGX Spark in this test, once again indicating its impressive efficiency and performance with this dense model and workload.

Of course, Qwen 3.8 27B doesn’t need 256GB (or even 128GB) of RAM to do its thing. To really stretch the legs of the Mac Studio, we chose two larger open models that are near the top of capability benchmarks right now: GLM-5.3 Flash and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017.

The M5 Ultra Mac Studio can natively fit quantized versions of these models (a four-bit quantization in the case of GLM 5.3 and a two-bit quantization courtesy of Unsloth for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash) in its 256GB of RAM, but there’s no way even those quantizations will fit on a single 128GB DGX Spark.

That’s where the clustering capability of Nvidia’s platform comes in. We’ve already seen how pairing up Sparks can scale up one’s local inference capacity, and that capability is critical when evaluating them against a Mac Studio like this.

But the rapidly rising prices of GB10 systems have frustrated our value analysis during the course of this testing. For just one example, Lenovo set all of its ThinkStation PGX systems to a “coming soon” status during our tests. And as we’ve already mentioned, the Dell Pro Precision GB10s we’re clustering here are an eye-popping $10K each. We have no reason to believe the rest of the GB10 systems on the market will be that much cheaper once this current volatility shakes out.

No matter how you slice it, getting even a pair of DGX Sparks with 4TB SSDs is going to end up costing quite a bit more than this single Mac Studio, even if $20K for a dual-Dell cluster is at the top end of the market. $15K or more is a relatively more reasonable benchmark, but even then, that’s 25% more money than a single M5 Ultra Studio.

We don't yet have end-to-end agentic tests for these larger models, but directionally, we can still consider prompt processing and decode performance to see which of these systems would deliver faster agentic turnaround.

Because of the limited choice of compatible inference engines for Apple Silicon, we have to use llama.cpp for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017. Worse, the Mac Studio can't hold both the DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 model weights and the DSpark speculative decoding model required to boost token output even with a two-bit quantization, so we’re forced to leave performance on the table.

In contrast, the DeepSeek V4.1 Flash serving recipe for our Spark cluster can take advantage of DSpark speculative decoding and the TensorFold inference engine, which can deliver higher performance than llama.cpp.

Even with MLX support, llama.cpp performance with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash on the Mac Studio is pokey next to the DGX Spark cluster. Between its slower prompt processing speed and unaugmented decoding, it would end up at a disadvantage in agentic work with this model.

The community two-Spark serving recipe from Mia’s AI Lab with TensorFold is a major asset for owners of those systems, even accounting for the bit of extra work involved in setting up and managing a Spark cluster.

Where the Mac running llama.cpp needs anywhere from 10 to 25 minutes to digest the longest context lengths we tested for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, the Spark cluster needs under five minutes at most to do the same jobs with this recipe — and the TensorFold inference engine is much faster on the decode side, too. If you want to run this model locally, a pair of Sparks is the clear winner right now.

With DeepSeek out of the way, let’s consider GLM 5.3 Flash, another leading open model for local intelligence. Because MTP with GLM 5.3 Flash doesn't require a separate drafting model, we can give it a decode boost on the Mac Studio without the memory headroom issues we saw with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash. For our Spark cluster, we chose to run GLM 5.3 Flash using a recipe cooked up by Mia's AI Lab.

GLM 5.3 Flash runs much better on llama.cpp than DeepSeek V4.1 Flash does, but even so, the DGX Spark cluster delivers much faster prompt processing times across the board. The M5 Ultra's memory bandwidth advantage means the Mac is delivering higher tokens per second on the decode side, but it’s likely that any agentic turn would still finish faster on the Spark cluster.

Because a TensorFold serving recipe is available for GLM 5.3 Flash on Apple Silicon, however, we can see just how much extra performance is available from an MLX-specific four-bit model quantization and a theoretically higher-performance inference engine.

This serving recipe turbocharges the Mac Studio’s performance compared to llama.cpp. Prompt processing times with TensorFold get much closer to those of the Spark cluster, and decode throughput is a startling three times faster, on average.

We expect (and can only hope) that improved inference engine support and serving recipes for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 on the M5 Ultra will arrive in time as community members unleash their coding agents on this hardware, because the performance potential is clearly there for Apple Silicon.

A single M5 Ultra Mac Studio can deliver incredible local AI performance and power efficiency versus a single DGX Spark when all the puzzle pieces fall into place. And that's not as absurd a comparison as it might seem.

Prices for unified memory systems are ballooning to the point that a single Dell Pro Precision GB10 system with a 4TB SSD is now over $10K, closing in on the $12K-ish price of the Mac Studio configuration that we tested. We expect those trends will only continue as the RAM and NAND supply crunch becomes more acute.

But our broader experience shows that the M5 Ultra's impressive performance isn't assured from every model and inference engine, especially from the bigger and highly intelligent models you'll want to put in 256GB of RAM.

This Mac's prompt processing speeds can really rip with llama.cpp and smaller models like Qwen 3.8 27B, but you really want to ensure that you have a higher-performance serving recipe at your disposal for bigger models like GLM 5.3 Flash and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017. The TensorFold engine runs GLM 5.3 Flash incredibly well, but it doesn’t support DeepSeek at all yet.

As we emphasized in our review of the M4 Max Mac Studio, memory bandwidth is just one factor in AI inference performance. You need to take the entire hardware-software stack into account in order to wholly judge the performance and model compatibility available from these extremely pricey unified memory setups.

Even as Apple closes the performance shortfalls of its hardware for AI inference, development and support around the MLX stack has a ways to go before it catches up with the maturity and performance, as well as the model and inference engine options, available on Nvidia hardware.

The enduring value of the DGX Spark platform beyond its raw performance and scalability is that the breadth and velocity of community software development and support around the platform has gotten to the point that you're virtually assured of a way to run a hot new model soon after its weights hit Hugging Face (at least assuming you have enough Sparks to cluster to provide the memory capacity necessary).

That assurance of community software support and ongoing development, along with the DRAM and NAND supply crunches, means high demand and ever higher prices for 128GB GB10 boxes. Two such Sparks with 4TB drives will run you an eye-watering $15K to $20K+, depending on the partner system and storage configuration you choose. And those prices are likely to continue rising, making a single Mac Studio a potentially appealing option as long as Apple doesn’t jack up its prices for these systems in turn.

If strong local AI performance is just one thing you need from a personal workstation, and you also want one of the world's fastest CPUs and a powerful GPU for general-purpose work in one well-rounded, efficient, and quiet compact chassis, the Mac Studio is a remarkable system.

If you can wait for software to catch up to this system’s incredible performance potential for local AI and don’t mind the possibility of narrower model and inference engine support compared to a DGX Spark setup, get in line, as lead times for these systems stretch into February 2027 as of this writing.

But if you're thinking about dropping well over $10K specifically for the purpose of building a local AI inference setup right now, perhaps for yourself or a small office, clustering a pair of Sparks gets you both the memory capacity and compute horsepower you need with the robust software support and broad model compatibility you’d expect for that outlay to be worthwhile. Those assurances may be worth the lower raw performance potential of such a cluster compared to the Mac Studio.

As the Senior Analyst, Graphics at Tom's Hardware, Jeff Kampman covers everything that has to do with graphics cards, gaming performance, and more. From integrated graphics processors to discrete graphics cards to the hyperscale installations powering our AI future, if it's got a GPU in it, Jeff is on it.

Benin vs Central African Republic - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By Byron David | 10 Nov 2026 13:19

Benin will seek to consolidate top spot in Group F of the 2027 Africa Cup of Nations qualifiers when they host the Central African Republic on Thursday, November 12.

After a relatively successful tournament in 2025, the hosts will hope to secure a ticket to next year's finals with a flurry of positive results.

Benin recorded their first-ever win at the Africa Cup of Nations last year, when they beat Botswana 1-0 in the group stage.

The Cheetahs qualified from the group for just the second time in their history, but Egypt put a stop to their run in the last-16 after a 3-1 victory in extra time.

Head coach Gernot Rohr will be hoping to inspire his troops to another major tournament, and they have started the qualification process with relative success.

Les Guepards won their most recent qualifier against Mauritania, after securing a point away from home against Burkina Faso in their opening match.

Rohr's charges sit top of the group, but only because they have scored one more goal than Burkina Faso in second.

There is no room for error here, as any slip-up from Benin during this international window could see either Mauritania or Burkina Faso pounce.

Benin's home form is positive on paper, undefeated in their last three such matches, securing two wins and a draw.

With this being their first recorded head-to-head with these opponents, the home side will want to lay down a marker, at least ahead of their away leg next week.

The Central African Republic are still searching for their first-ever appearance at an AFCON tournament, having come close before in 2021 and 2023.

However, Les Fauves have not made the best of starts during this qualification campaign, as they suffered back-to-back defeats and head to Benin lying bottom of the group.

The worrying factor for the visitors is that they have only just appointed Abdelaziz Kerkache to take over the head coach role from Eloge Enza Yamissi.

The Moroccan has his work cut out for him, especially with his appointment coming less than a week before this crucial trip to Benin.

Kerkache inherits a team that has not won a match since October last year, and won just one of their last seven away matches, in which they lost five.

The home side will be without their captain Steve Mounie as he battles an injury, but there is good news for 16-year-old Parfait Faton, who received his maiden call-up to the national team.

Rohr will likely maintain faith with Marcel Dandjinou between the sticks, behind a back-four that could comprise Charlemagne Azongnitode, Yohan Roche, Mohamed Tijani and Tamimou Ouorou.

Sessi D'Almeida and Hassane Imourane will form part of the engine room, while Rodolfo Aloko is almost certain of a starting berth after his brace against Mauritania.

Venuste Baboula is a doubt for the visitors, as he is nursing an ankle injury, but perhaps the reverse fixture might be more suitable for him.

Karkache will still be learning about his squad, which means he may opt for much of the team that started in their 1-0 defeat to Mauritania.

Consequently, the Cheetahs are likely to consolidate their spot in the group with three points.

The King of the Monsters is back, and bigger than ever. “Godzilla Minus Zero,” the sequel to 2023’s Oscar-winning “Godzilla Minus One,” stomped into U.S. theaters on November 6, and it's been attracting kaiju-hungry viewers ever since.

Picking up in 1949, two years after the events of the first film, the sequel continues the story of the Shikishima family in a shattered post-war Japan as they face a new threat from the towering kaiju.

The film was shot specifically for IMAX, the first Japanese production ever to do so. It’s playing in theaters right now, but how long before you can watch it from the comfort of your own couch?

Since it just hit theaters, “Godzilla Minus Zero” isn’t available on any of the best streaming services just yet. Here’s the good news, though: based on how its predecessor rolled out, we can make a solid guess about when you’ll be able to rent, buy, and stream it at home.

If you’re curious about when to start planning for a giant lizard-stomping movie night, we’ve got the answers right here. Keep reading to find out.

North American distributor GKIDS hasn’t announced a digital release date yet, but if “Godzilla Minus Zero” follows the typical pattern for a theatrical release, it should hit premium video-on-demand (PVOD) about a month after it opens, at which point you can rent or buy it on platforms like Amazon and Apple TV.

Counting forward from its November 6 release, that points to a digital debut sometime in December 2026.

Sign up to the weekly Tom's Streaming Guide to stay in the loop with the latest releases and top recommendations across your favorite streaming services. We’ve got you covered with a complete guide on what’s worth watching.

Physical media is a different story. It typically follows a few weeks after the digital release. “Godzilla Minus One” got a well-regarded 4K UHD and Blu-ray release, so its sequel is likely to get the same treatment.

That means a physical release for “Godzilla Minus Zero” around January 2027 seems like a reasonable bet, though nothing has been confirmed just yet. Since it was shot for IMAX, a 4K disc will be the closest most people get to that presentation at home.

As for streaming the movie with a subscription service, our strongest clue for a release date is how the first film was handled. “Godzilla Minus One” landed on Netflix in the U.S. about six months after its theatrical release, and with Netflix having become the streaming home for that movie, its sequel seems likely to follow suit.

If it sticks to the same roughly six-month timeline, “Godzilla Minus Zero” could reach Netflix sometime around May 2027. A ways off, yes, but good things are worth waiting for.

That said, nothing about the streaming date has been officially confirmed, so the exact platform and timing could still change. For now, the only way to see Godzilla’s latest rampage is on the biggest screen you can find. And you can still enjoy the other 30+ movies in the "Godzilla" franchise while waiting.

“Godzilla Minus Zero” is a direct sequel to 2023’s “Godzilla Minus One,” so you'll want to make sure you're caught up before watching this movie. It was set in the ashes of World War II and followed Kōichi Shikishima (Ryunosuke Kamiki), a former kamikaze pilot consumed by guilt after failing to act during an earlier Godzilla attack.

Back in Tokyo, he builds a family with Noriko (Minami Hamabe) and an orphaned child, only for a mutated Godzilla to rise from the sea and lay waste to the city. A group of civilian veterans ultimately got together to take the monster down and succeeded, so you'll want to watch that play out before diving into this movie.

“Minus Zero” picks the story back up in 1949, two years after those events. The Shikishima family has started to rebuild amid a Japan still struggling to recover, but the peace doesn’t last, and Godzilla returns to threaten the country once again.

With the memory of the last devastation still fresh, a disaster-response team scrambles to prepare for the renewed menace, and the Shikishimas are pulled right back into the fight for survival. And yes, it's just as epic in scale as the last movie, so you'll want to catch this one whether you see it at home or on the big screen.

England face Croatia in the penultimate game of the Nations League group stage, which will see captain Harry Kane become the most-capped player in Three Lions’ history.

Croatia head to Wembley, with their mauling at the hands of the Three Lions’ in Rijeka last month still fresh in the memory.

Thomas Tuchel’s side put seven past their hosts without reply, as Harry Kane and Bukayo Saka bagged braces. There were further goals from Jude Bellingham, Anthony Gordon, and Morgan Rogers as England wiped the floor with Croatia.

England are poised to advance to the next stage of the Nations League but currently via a second-placed finish, which would set up a quarter final with a big hitter such as France or Portugal.

Victory for England at Wembley would likely set-up a last-game decider with Spain, the winner of that match topping the group.

'Markets bottom on bad news' maxim rings true as chip stocks rally amid Nvidia scandal

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

"Markets bottom on bad news," is one of those smart-sounding rules of thumb that proves true just often enough to remain a Wall Street cliché in good standing.

Did the brutal weeks-long punishment of AI stocks culminate last week and reverse higher Monday after a pileup of adverse headlines? In the past week, American voters resoundingly opposed data-center construction across much of the country and Anthropic lowered its target IPO valuation just before a sex-and-espionage scandal struck the one-man embodiment of the entire AI economy, Nvidia's Jensen Huang.

The story of a market taking a cue from a crescendo of bad news after a nasty setback to stage a resounding bounce holds together well enough for now, after Monday's 3.4% jump in the Nasdaq-100 and 7% rip in the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index (SOX), even as Nvidia shed 4.3%.

Based purely on the tactical evidence, the tech-hardware complex came into the week pretty well washed out and primed to bounce. Numerous trading desks reported historically rare intensity of net selling in the group by institutions into last Thursday's risk purge. The Nasdaq-100 was as oversold on several metrics as it had been at the March 30 Iran-selloff low. The CBOE S&P 500 Volatility Index has put in a nice spike of the sort usually associated with a passing storm, shooting up to 28 and then receding below 20.

The recent dump in the Nasdaq and uptick in risk aversion, "had many elements of capitulation," says Laurent Laskowski, options strategist at Baird Strategas: "record options activity, a sharp spike in volatility, skew and correlations, an inverted VIX curve, and forced liquidations…The setup, therefore, is one where the market is seeing the [downside] left tail narrowing while the [upside] right tail is fattening."

Several technical-analysis gurus (including Macro Risk Advisors' John Kolovos, whose work is cited below) argue the market hasn't quite served up a "fat pitch" to buy the pullback aggressively. Maybe so. But FanGraphs calculates that in Major League Baseball only about 7% of all pitches are in the very center of the strike zone. While those fat pitches result in some 23% of all home runs hit, the other three-quarters of balls that fly over the wall come with less-perfect setups around the edges of the zone.

I wouldn't argue too strenuously against this tech-centered rally carrying on for a bit, for these reasons as well as the fact that valuations have become far less demanding. Last week, I noted Big Tech had not to that point responded to moderately stressed conditions but was getting closer, and "the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market." Monday's blip higher could be a start, with plenty still to prove.

But this AI-driven part of the market has lately shown itself less worthy of investor trust, leaving enough hints of structural damage to the bull case that faith can't be restored right away.

In a late-June column, I asked, "Was May 14 a consequential moment for this market, at least tactically?" That date, nearly six months ago, was when the S&P 500 spurted higher to reach the 7500 level for the first time; the index has never reached escape velocity since and in fact crossed above 7500 again just yesterday.

May 14 was when highly touted AI-chip upstart Cerebras came public and its shares more than doubled to a high of $386, as President Trump and China's leader were reported to have made progress on ending the Iran war. Since then, Cerebras has collapsed to $97, the semiconductor group is off 17% and the Iran conflict continues.

A month later the SpaceX IPO was another apparent culmination of New Era tech enthusiasm; it might not have been an important peak in AI excitement, but if it were the market action since would possibly resemble what we've seen since.

Perhaps a half-year reckoning period during which the market has refused to pay up for 30% annual earnings growth for two straight quarters is enough to build a new base constructed on more modest expectations. I've been focused on late-cycle tremors unsettling markets in the form of peak earnings growth, peak capex, peak index concentration, a tighter Federal Reserve and credit flareups. This all explains the market's multi-month stall, and they remain.

But it's important to remain open to the chance that these could end up amounting to mere mid-cycle turbulence, as Morgan Stanley chief investment officer Mike Wilson has been arguing.

First, though, the tape will need to show more progress than a nice, broad single-day comeback. Another rule of thumb holds that a rebound rally needs at least to carry above the 20-day moving average to prove that it's not merely a reflexive dead-cat bounce. This would require a 1.3% gain from Monday's close for the S&P 500, 3.7% for the Nasdaq-100 and 9% for the SOX.

Without pretending any clear sense of what the various Nvidia investigations will mean for its China sales, leadership or long-term earnings power, we can say for sure that the scandal arrived at a fairly opportune time for the market.

Valuations have been pounded to multi-year lows on persistent worry that current profits are unsustainable and the group remains vulnerable to any slowdown in data-center builds. This allowed, for a day, every other semiconductor stock to whistle higher as Nvidia lost some $200 billion in market cap on a blistering $80 billion in dollar-volume traded in the stock.

The S&P 500 as a whole, of course, has grown optically cheaper, too, with semis bolstering the projected earnings and investors declining to give the group credit for their sustainability.

The issue remains that less of those earnings are set to manifest as free cash flow as the hyperscalers spend manically. As this chart comparing the valuation gauges shows, the market looks a good deal less cheap on free cash flow than earnings.

A proper bull case for the market from here might be that the likes of Alphabet, Amazon, Meta and Microsoft will resume harvesting free cash flow, on which investors might place a higher multiple than they're willing to bestow on those hot but hard-to-trust chip makers. Not yet a provable argument.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "While sentiment is dire, our concern is that technology is in a 'bad oversold' condition, where seductive bounces fail to produce lasting lows. Instead, the broader equity market may need to follow technology lower, with a more widespread capitulation ultimately needed to wash out weak hands and establish a durable bottom."

-This enjoyable and illuminating review in the New Yorker of James Gleick's "The Telephone: A New History" inevitably summons parallels between the advent of telecommunications and the current AI buildout. Huge privately financed infrastructure buildout, destabilizing new habits of interaction with technology and constant calls for government regulation in exchange for near-monopoly power. The book cites an AT&T ad from 1909 calling telephones "tools of 'civilization . . . one of the most powerful influences for broadening human intelligence.'"

-Moody's Analytics economist Mark Zandi has a helpful thread suggesting AI is so far adding more jobs than it's costing the U.S. economy. The main reasons are sobering though, with most gains coming from data-center construction and wealth creation for those who own AI-dependent stocks. Cold comfort for information-economy job seekers.

-Last week's election triggering a Democratic takeover of Congress brought comparisons to the 1994 "Contract with America" Republican sweep for its magnitude and message. That election, in Bill Clinton's first term, highlighted deep societal fissures and economic instability.

With hindsight, many view the '90s as a placid era of consensus and prosperity, but between the '94 backlash election, constant corporate downsizing, scare-mongering over Federal debt levels, the 1995 Oklahoma City bombing and discomfort with technological change, that decade hit many of today's narrative beats. This Chicago Fed paper, "The decline of job security in the 1990s: Displacement, anxiety, and their effect on wage growth" can fill in some blanks for those not around for it.

Happy fifth anniversary to the peak of a pandemic speculative-tech bull market. Not quite two months before the S&P 500 hit a top in January 2022 as the Fed prepared to choke off surging inflation, perhaps by causing a recession, several key retail-trader-driven subsectors peaked.

The third week of November 2021 was the relative-performance peak in Tesla, as well as the absolute top in Coinbase and the Wisdom Tree Cloud Computing ETF (WCLD).

The five-year lookback period is one of the few time spans shy of 25 years over which the Nasdaq Composite has not outperformed the S&P 500. It's a plus that the broad market can withstand such internal speculative blowoffs, though also a good reminder that the most fun pockets of the market don't stay that way forever.

North Korea Hackers Launches Elon Musk Memecoins to Launder $6.4B Binance Hack

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

North Korea backed hackers have created Elon Musk themed memecoins to launder stolen crypto in the $6.4 billion Binance hack. The hackers are using fake trading activity to disguise the movement of stolen funds.

One of the tokens featured Musk beneath an “Elon 2028 President” banner. Five others carried cartoon animals, crude jokes and familiar dog-coin branding. All six were created within three days and quickly abandoned.

Blockchain analytics firm Elliptic identified 117,000 NEAR tokens, worth $430,000, passing through the homemade coins between November 5 and November 7. The operation offers another look at how North Korea’s crypto laundering networks are adapting their techniques to move stolen assets.

Elliptic identified six newly created tokens on the NEAR blockchain: FUK, OLCCL, MEILI, MMLI, DABI and SINU. Each token was paired with NEAR in its own decentralized exchange liquidity pool. The hackers then used one group of wallets to purchase the tokens with stolen NEAR before another group sold them back.

North Korea’s launderers are creating their own meme coins to move funds stolen from Binance.Between November 5 and 7, they launched at least six tokens on NEAR, dressed up with meme-coin logos such as a monkey in a pink bow and a duck in front of a US flag. Stolen NEAR went…

This separated the buying and selling wallets, preventing the stolen funds from moving directly between them. The transactions consequently resembled ordinary crypto trading, with the receiving wallets appearing to collect proceeds from successful memecoin trades.

Blockchain investigators could still follow the money. Elliptic found that almost all trading activity came from wallets connected to the laundering operation. Outside buyers accounted for less than 1% of one token’s trading volume.

The operation was also remarkably inexpensive. Creating each token cost just as low as 25 NEAR, or worth around $90. Five of the six token creators received funding from wallets that had already participated in earlier token sales.

The tokens were effectively disposable. All six stopped trading by November 8, with the creators of four withdrawing remaining liquidity.

The technique does not erase the transaction trail. Instead, it introduces additional steps that slow investigators trying to connect stolen funds with their eventual destinations. The firm previously identified a similar approach following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025. North Korean launderers moved at least $24 million through homemade tokens using the same basic mechanism.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

The memecoin operation represents only a small fraction of the cryptocurrency stolen from Binance. The approximately $6.43 billion was taken from the exchange on October 29, making it the largest crypto theft ever recorded. Binance estimated its losses at $6.3 billion.

The FBI subsequently attributed the attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group. Investigators have identified multiple laundering techniques since the breach, including cryptocurrency swaps, cross-chain transfers, and movements through decentralized finance platforms.

Approximately $2.85 billion has been frozen, representing around 44% of the stolen assets. This leaves billions requiring further tracing as investigators race to identify wallets and freeze assets wherever possible.

🚨BINANCE HACK: $6.3B IMPACT, $3B FROZEN ON-CHAIN, LOSSES FULLY COVERED!Binance’s initial investigation suggests a compromised DevOps engineer’s laptop allowed an attacker to access a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions.No evidence of a wallet… pic.twitter.com/NA5taJLmv5

The use of North Korean hackers and memecoins adds another disposable layer to this operation. Rather than relying exclusively on conventional exchanges or direct wallet transfers, hackers can manufacture trading activity around virtually worthless tokens.

Meanwhile, Bitcoin’s expanding Layer 2 ecosystem is attracting developers looking to introduce faster transactions and programmable financial applications to the network.

Bitcoin Hyper is among the projects pursuing this approach, combining a Solana Virtual Machine-based execution environment with Bitcoin settlement. Its proposed infrastructure targets near-instant transaction finality alongside decentralized applications and DeFi functionality.

The project currently records more than $33.2 million raised in its presale, with HYPER priced at just $0.0136877 and staking rewards of 35% APY.

As crypto infrastructure continues to evolve, the race between increasingly sophisticated laundering networks and blockchain investigators is unlikely to slow down.

Military Member Calls Alaska Airlines Employee “Anti-Military” Over Denied Free Upgrade

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A military member and social media influencer has taken to Instagram to complain about a recent interaction with an Alaska Airlines employee, where he asked for a free upgrade, and was denied. He’s accusing an employee at the airline of being anti-military. Is that a fair complaint, or is this being blown out of proportion?

A military member posted a 2min35sec video online, outlining his experience with an Alaska Airlines representative at Portland International Airport (PDX), which he claims was his first time encountering an “anti-military” employee at the airline. To summarize his version of events:

You can see the video for yourself below. As you’d expect, this guy’s followers are mostly military related, so the 1,300+ comments on the post are almost entirely in agreement with the person who posted the video.

First let me say that I of course respect and appreciate the service of those who are in the military (even if I fundamentally disagree with the extent to which we get into conflicts that accomplish nothing — that’s the fault of our elected leaders, and not the fault of those who put their lives on the line).

Of course if the Alaska Airlines employee in question was rude, that’s not acceptable, and people expect polite service. Now, I’m a little skeptical of how much of a direct transcript this was, rather than how this guy chose to interpret it. That includes things like him suggesting that the Alaska employee accused him of lying. If that were the case, that’s unacceptable, but again, it’s not clear if that was actually said, or just how he was made to feel.

All that being said, this guy sort of strikes me as being rather entitled. Airlines are extremely generous with active duty military members, as they should be. The airline has a page that outlines military and veteran benefits, and upgrades isn’t among those perks. The airline offers all kinds of other things, ranging from special fares, to lounge access, to priority boarding. But it seems a little greedy to then be like “oh, they do all these things, but they should do more” (since he chooses to reference this page).

But here’s my bigger issue with this guy’s approach. I don’t think there’s anything wrong with asking for an upgrade, but be ready to be shot down. However, I feel like his approach is more one of entitlement than “it can’t hurt to ask.” Why do I say that?

The very example he gives is that for all these times he flies Alaska, either they say “sure, we’ll upgrade you,” or they say “sorry, it’s full, we can’t upgrade you.” In other words, he assumes he’s entitled to the upgrade as long as there are open seats, which just isn’t the policy. If he’s going to be rejected for something he’s not entitled to, he wants to be lied to, rather than told “sorry, that’s not our policy.”

And again, he emphasizes how he has no level of entitlement, but also then argues by pointing out that he has been upgraded for free in the past. It all sounds to me a little like being caught with a liquid that’s too large at the TSA checkpoint, and then trying to argue with them, and say “well last time they let me through.” Well, that’s fine, but that’s not the policy.

So my take here is that airline employees should of course always be professional. I think it’s perfectly fine to say “I’m sorry, that’s not our policy,” which is something this guy seems to take issue with. If the employee did actually accuse him of lying about being upgraded in the past, that’s of course not acceptable. But I’m also not convinced that actually happened.

A military member has accused an Alaska Airlines employee of being anti-military, after being denied a free upgrade to a seat with more legroom. He argues that there’s no harm in asking, but also seems to think that the only acceptable rejection is a claim that the cabin is full.

While Alaska offers lots of perks to military members (as they should), upgrades isn’t among them. Asking once is one thing, but I do find it a bit entitled to essentially believe that you’re entitled to the seats as long as they’re empty, and then to argue if you’re not upgraded.

What do you make of this “anti-military” Alaska employee?

As someone who is active duty, I'm upset the guy posted the video. Airlines are generous, so are credit card companies. The guy is entitled.

“he claims that nine times out of 10, he gets moved up” So this was one of those one time out of 10 that he wasn’t moved up.

Those who serve (key word: serve) in the military deserve our appreciation and respect. Their military ID is not, however, the equivalent of a premium credit card that comes with perks and benefits beyond what is directly prescribed by the US government. Someone who has chosen to serve is a laudable career decision, but it is a career decision. It is a job for which they are compensated. That job does not and should not come with the entitlement or expectation of early boarding, upgrades or freebies beyond what their pay package includes. Airlines can, of course, do whatever they choose to do, though it always feels like pandering to me.
